Biological Heritability

Genetic information is more stable, less variable across generations

Slower pace of genomic change and evolutionary innovation

Genome integrity and function easier to maintain

Units: species, other taxa

Genetic information is less stable, more variable across generations

Faster pace of genomic change and evolutionary innovation

Genome integrity and function harder to maintain

Units: genes, functions

Horizontal evolution accelerates the transmission of DNA and all that it encodes or influences. This includes proteins, immunities, body parts, behaviors, and more. Horizontal evolution events are mutations, and, as is well known, most mutations disrupt the lives of organisms, long honed by natural selection, in ways that are not helpful. But some of these mutations may be advantageous by chance or in changed environments, allowing those mutations to be selected for and to succeed over time. Horizontal evolution also brings potential for genetic variation of greater consequence, compared with vertical, parent-to-progeny change. This includes bringing genes and functions that are more divergent, or simply absent, from those of the recipient species. The horizontal sharing of heritable materials and the traits they influence adds elements of speed and unpredictability to organismal change, beyond the confines of vertical evolution from parents to progeny.

In many ways and for many groups, the living world is more fluid than previously imagined. Ridley notes: “The secret sauce that leads to innovation is freedom. Freedom to exchange, experiment, imagine, invest and fail.”10 In the context of heritability, the mechanisms of horizontal evolution can be that freedom and secret sauce for innovation.

Mutation and Creativity

There is an interesting paradox here. Horizontal evolution can be a secret sauce for innovation. Yet, events of horizontal evolution begin as mutations, which are also known to be errors in copying genetic materials. It’s inauspicious, but it’s also how evolution works. Life’s nearly infinite innovations all begin as mutations. Wings, brains, immune systems, sex, multicellularity, and eusociality—as in bees with sterile workers—all trace their origins to errors in copying and transferring of DNA sequences as they pass from parents to offspring or donors to recipients. Mutations are often neutral or detrimental in their effects on organisms. There are countless ways to make already-functioning genes and organisms worse, and relatively few to make them better. However, without mutations, change by natural selection is hobbled. Life without mutation would be caught in a 3.8-billion-year loop, a bad version of the movie Groundhog Day. So, let’s admit, mutation is also good.

Given its link to error, mutation receives much less attention as a creative force in evolution than natural selection. Darwin’s and Wallace’s seminal works in the nineteenth century were preoccupied with making the case for the powers of natural selection, shaping nature’s glory from an ocean of mostly minor variants. But they were unaware of molecular biology or then-invisible genomes and their potential for quick and consequential change. The study of bacteria was only beginning, and an interoperability of heritable materials across very different species could not be foreseen.

One of the oldest continuing controversies in evolutionary biology is whether evolution is primarily driven by selection or mutation. Personally, I think the controversy is more imagined than real. Nearly everyone agrees that both are important and mutually dependent in the flow of evolution. However, selection has often been seen as primary when the mutations being discussed are small-scale mutations, often changes of single nucleotide positions in a string of DNA. But as we’ve learned more about the full range of mutations that happen, and the range of biases in mutation that organisms show, the idea of mutation as creative force in evolution has been expanding. We now know of myriad kinds of DNA duplications, from single nucleotides to parts of genes to whole genomes. There are DNA insertions and losses, as well as mutations in chromosome structure to contend with, along with all the mutations arising from the material horizontal evolution mechanisms (hybridization, microbial recombination, endosymbioses, and horizontal gene transfer) that are the focus of this book. Mutation as mere error is too narrow. Mutations are also unwitting experiments in which DNA sequence alterations might play out as sustaining innovation and adaptivity.

Masatoshi Nei, a pioneer in evolutionary genetics, argued that most functional genes are constrained—they can’t change or they lose their function—and that rare constraint-breaking mutations are the actual drivers of evolution, more so than selection. Further, he suggests the existence of suitable habitats without species, poorly adapted species, and species extinctions argues against the power of natural selection and small mutations (single nucleotide changes within DNA sequences) to accomplish what evolution has wrought.11

Horizontal evolution, when it persists, tends to be more disruptive than change at single DNA sequence positions. Processes of horizontal evolution operate on a different and larger scale. Hybridization can mix the full set of genes from different species. Joining can add an entire genome, yielding a composite taxon, and horizontal gene transfer can bring full genes and sets of genes that are new to a species. This results in not only the presence and potential of new genes but new gene combinations as well. Species that are only distantly related can share genetic material, and become integrated in this way.

Sets of genes may be gained by horizontal evolution that had been shaped by natural selection in their previous host to work together. This also means innovation can happen faster. Gaining photosynthesis is a bigger and faster change compared with a single nucleotide substitution. Although these features may reduce functional stability of the organisms receiving genetic materials horizontally, they may also provide, by chance, variation that is useful and adaptive, perhaps when the environment is variable. That variability may include unusual weather or new competitors, predators, parasites, and pathogens. Changing knowledge of the scope and pace of mutation to include horizontal evolution events lends it a greater and more creative role in evolution than once thought. Imagine that some of your day-to-day mistakes yield brilliant, unexpected consequences. You go to the wrong airport terminal, miss your flight, and then meet the love of your life while cursing and killing time.

Four Billion Years of Horizontal Evolution

To appreciate the scope and significance of horizontal evolution across all life’s history, let’s consider horizontal evolution events, following an evolutionary timeline. This includes overviews of horizontal evolution in life’s origins and across the two primary groups of organisms, bacteria and archaea, and their combined offshoot, eukaryotes. I’ll call attention to horizontal evolution’s influence on several innovations deemed to be major transitions in the history of life.12 These are the transition to multicellularity, use of oxygen and sunlight, and sexual reproduction. My purpose here is to exemplify the support for evolution’s new narrative.

Life’s Origins and Early Evolution

Life arose about 3.8 billion years ago following hundreds of millions of years of chemical interactions in which carbon compounds and water were key players. The natural properties of chemicals, together with the constraints of physics, led to self-replicating chemical compounds and the emergence of what would become cell membranes made with lipids, which, importantly, do not dissolve in water. These features enabled the natural evolution of single-celled life ancestral to all species. Although many details and questions remain to be resolved, numerous steps in this process of life’s origins have been observed in both nature and lab experiments.13

Microbiologist Carl Woese first developed the scenario in which this earliest time in life’s evolution, prior to the divergences of archaea, bacteria, and eukaryotes, was dominated by horizontal gene transfer. He suggested that the ancestor for those primary lineages “was communal, a loosely knit, diverse conglomeration of primitive cells that evolved as a unit.… [A]ny lineages evolving barriers to acquisition or expression of foreign genes would be left behind in the evolutionary progression toward modern life. Lateral gene transfer of this kind … would completely dominate the primitive evolutionary dynamic.”14 He coined the term “Darwinian threshold” for the time when life transitioned from primarily horizontal evolution, with widespread sharing of genes, to greater vertical evolution with more or most genes moving between single-celled parents and their offspring as cell design became more complex and less flexible.15 Although this idea is plausible, there is no evidence to support it, and this is as might be expected. No species with enough permanence to leave a genealogical trace could exist on the early side of any such threshold owing to the ephemeral nature of the “conglomeration.” We are left with a reasonable yet untestable supposition, at least for now. The important roles of horizontal evolution and decentralization in life’s origins, along with the involvement of viruses, will be considered in more detail in chapter 4.

Bacteria and Archaea

Life’s earliest divergences gave rise to our two largest groups of single-celled organisms, bacteria and archaea, between 3.5 and 4 billion years ago.16 These two groups combined represent an estimated 78 percent of Earth’s species today (excluding viruses; see table 1.3). They are ubiquitous across Earth’s land and waters, including some found in extreme environments such as hot springs, hydrothermal vents, the Earth’s crust (more than a mile below the ocean floor), and extremes of salinity, desiccation, cold, and radioactivity. They also compose the microbiome residing within individual humans and all other eukaryotes. There are even some bacteria living it up within other bacteria.17 No doubt about it, they have been wildly successful in diversifying and adapting to Earth’s many opportunities.

Bacteria and archaea are asexual. There are no reproductive cells, like eggs or sperm, and no individuals are deemed to be males or females. But does that mean microbes are barred somehow from the benefits of sex? Must they abstain from the genetic diversity brought when males and females contribute their differences to offspring? Is diversity-limiting clonal reproduction their only option? Of course not. Genetic recombination happens across all life-forms but is particularly frequent in bacteria and archaea (and viruses), and is analogous to sexual reproduction, and potentially hybridization, among eukaryotes. We just don’t call it sex. There are three basic mechanisms for bacterial and archaeal recombination, but their impacts in genetic mixing are similar. They are (1) transformation, importing DNA from the environment; (2) transduction, transferring DNA among bacteria by viruses; and (3) conjugation, transferring DNA by cell-to-cell contact. All forms of recombination increase genetic diversity within those groups.

Recombination by bacteria and archaea is estimated to be nearly as old as the groups themselves, based on estimated ages of relevant genes.18 This places extensive horizontal evolution in the early stages of life’s evolution. The potential for early microbial life-forms to share, recombine, miscopy, and share again their genetic materials, including suites of genes honed to accomplish particular functions, makes for a powerful engine of innovation. This can result in new species with new capabilities. New genetic material and new combinations of genes may serve organisms well when they find themselves in altered environments, or when new competitors and pathogens threaten their populations. Recombination is also commonplace among close relatives within bacteria and archaea.

Like recombination, horizontal gene transfer has occurred within bacteria and within archaea throughout their evolution. The bacterial species Mesotoga infera provides a good example of the extensive horizontal gene transfer and decentralization of genetic materials that can be identified in living individuals. Identification of horizontal gene transfer is done by comparing genomes from M. infera with representatives of all the major groups of bacteria. Individuals donating gene copies to M. infera via horizontal gene transfer, over time, span the full diversity of bacteria, as described by the arrows in figure 2.2.19 Let that sink in. A rough analogy would be if a human genome were found to have unique genes from diverse eukaryotes, including a selection animals, plants, and fungi.

Those diverse gene origins within Mesotoga infera denote decentralization of its genome over time within bacteria. But what is the average extent of genome decentralization across bacterial species? Another recent study estimates that 30 to 50 percent of bacterial genomes on average stem from horizontal gene transfer events.20 The horizontally transferred genes and the potential innovations they represent are functionally important in the daily lives of their bacterial recipients. They encode genes used in metabolizing food; copying and translating DNA into proteins; and the defense, signaling, and movement of bacterial cells. The transferred genes, and the innovations they entail, matter. Estimates of the amounts of horizontal gene transfer represented within genomes will change as tools improve and more genomes are sequenced. But the existence of meaningful decentralization of genetic materials and the resulting integration among diverse groups of bacteria is clear.

Archaea show similar trends to bacteria in the frequency of horizontal evolution. Comparisons of archaeal genes and genomes across diverse groups show that an average of 12 to 22 percent of genes are the result of past horizontal gene transfer events (figure 2.3). As with bacteria, these estimates for archaea span important categories of gene function and innovation, indicating their integral roles in the structure and maintenance of living archaeal individuals.21

Eukaryotes

Next within the sequence of divergence among life’s primary groups is the divergence within archaea that gave rise to eukaryotes about 1.5 billion years ago. Both archaea and eukaryotes have continued a degree of genetic sharing with bacteria from their first divergence up to recent times. Eukaryotes is our group, including an estimated 22 percent of Earth’s species. These are the animals, plants, fungi, protists, and their immediate ancestors.

Diversification within eukaryotes began with small organisms, now long gone, that we would consider to be protists, the name for eukaryotes that are not animals, plants, or fungi. Though still controversial, some experts posit the early diverging protists were “almost certainly” single-celled predators able to swallow individual bacteria or archaea.22 This ability to ingest organic material as food is a hallmark of eukaryotes, distinguishing them from bacteria and archaea. Three different eukaryotic protists caught in the act of predation are shown in figure 2.4. Microscopic observations of protist predation are relatively recent, and as the behavior of more protists is scrutinized, predation is turning out to be more common than imagined.

MAKING USE OF OXYGEN AND SUNLIGHT

One of the most consequential lineage joining events for eukaryotes was the endosymbiosis (one organism living inside another) giving rise to mitochondria, about 1.8 billion years ago. The lineages that joined were an archaea species ancestral to eukaryotes and a bacteria species from a species-rich group called the Alphaproteobacteria.23 To set the stage, the amount of oxygen in Earth’s air had been increasing owing to the activities of photosynthesizing bacteria. Most life-forms at the time were anaerobic, and unable to cope with the increasing oxygen levels. Some ancient Alphaproteobacteria, however, evolved the ability to metabolize oxygen, breaking it down and creating a useful energy source (adenosine triphosphate, or ATP). Over time, anaerobic archaea that engulfed and joined with an incipient oxygen metabolizer would be an evolutionary winner, giving rise to eukaryotic cells containing and powered by mitochondria.24 This primary endosymbiosis, yielding mitochondria, has been of fundamental importance to the evolution of nearly all eukaryotic species, helping them diversify and colonize a richly oxygenated Earth.

Similarly, about 1.5 billion years ago, ancient protists feeding on photosynthesizing cyanobacteria led to another joining of lineages. This time the endosymbionts were cyanobacteria, which eventually gave rise to chloroplasts. The resulting protists now had both mitochondria and chloroplasts. In turn, these protists gave rise to three different photosynthesizing groups: (1) all land plants and green algae, (2) red algae, and (3) the glaucophytes, a small but distinctive group of freshwater algae.

In various protists, there were a series of secondary, tertiary, and higher-order endosymbioses, when a protist having a chloroplast was swallowed by another protist, sometimes also having a chloroplast, which was swallowed by yet another, and so on, in Russian doll fashion. These higher-order endosymbioses appear to have happened on two occasions involving green algae endosymbionts, and on two or more occasions involving red algae as endosymbionts. Figure 2.5 provides a visual take on some of the serial mergers that have happened in eukaryotes on their way from antiquity to the present. This is an active area of research and details for these and similar events are still being evaluated.

Researchers have recently suggested an additional layering of horizontal evolution in the story of the endosymbiotic origin of chloroplasts. The evidence supporting the suggestion is extensive horizontal gene transfer from bacteria in the Chlamydiae group into the nuclear genome of the initial eukaryotic host of the incipient chloroplast. The metabolic functions of these genes may have been vital precursors in the later success of the endosymbiotic cyanobacteria. If this view holds up, one kind of horizontal evolution will have facilitated another kind of horizontal evolution (lineage merger).25 Bacterial species in the group Chlamydiae have proven quite adaptable and are ubiquitous symbionts of protists. Unfortunately for us, species from this same group are responsible for chlamydia, a sexually transmitted infection.

Species-joining events enabling photosynthesis (chloroplasts) and oxygen metabolism (mitochondria) seem, in hindsight, inevitable for life on Earth. Abundant sunlight and oxygen were shining opportunities for innovation. Mutation, selection, and time naturally found them. The origin of all plants is derived from the lineage-joining events responsible for their signature feature: the chloroplasts enabling photosynthesis. All the innovations in plant form and function, from towering redwood trees to creeping vines and small flowerless mosses, rely and riff on the early innovation of making food from sunlight. Diversity for all eukaryotes is similarly linked to the endosymbiotic origins of mitochondria. The innovation and ability to convert oxygen into energy that mitochondria provide is requisite for their survival. The only known eukaryotes without mitochondria are single-celled flagellates in the genus Monocercomonoides, which are descended from groups having mitochondria. The ancient lineage-joining events yielding chloroplasts and mitochondria alone indicate the catalytic powers of horizontal evolution reverberating across all eukaryotes.

MULTICELLULARITY

The evolution of multicellular organisms from single-celled organisms is one of life’s major transitions. It allowed for specialization of different functions in different cells, as well as for increases in organism size and longevity. And it happened on multiple occasions. Estimates for the number of times depends on how multicellularity is defined. If we restrict multicellularity to mean organisms with cell-to-cell communication and cooperation, then we can count 11 separate origins. Once each in slime molds, which are protists, and in animals; three times in fungi; and twice in each of the major photosynthetic groups: plants, red algae, and the Stramenopiles, the latter also being protists, including flagellates and diatoms.26

The origins of multicellularity involve the evolution of many complex features, and it appears different groups of organisms traveled a variety of different paths in getting there. There is no broad agreement about which mechanisms have been most important across the multiple origins of multicellularity, and relatively little can be said about the potential role of horizontal evolution. Among many possible influencing factors, some researchers think it is likely that the same predatory behavior in protists discussed above for endosymbioses and leading to chloroplasts was an influence on the multiple origins of multicellularity. Recent lab experiments have shown that predation on groups of single-celled algae by the fearsome single-celled protist Paramecium tetraurelia results, repeatedly, in the coevolution of multicellular algae, with multicellular prey being larger and more difficult to swallow.27 Other researchers point to horizontal coevolution between unicellular hosts and pathogens as a potential key influencing factor, as computer-based simulations of competitive host-pathogen “arms races” indicate benefits of being multicellular over unicellular.28

SEXUAL REPRODUCTION

Sex is decentralizing too. Whether plant, animal, fungal, or protist, both males and females contribute half of their genes to the embryo. Those genes are then shuffled in the embryo and that creates new gene combinations for selection to act upon. Given the biological imperative for individuals to pass on their genes, and the alternative of donating all their genes by being asexual, rather than half by being sexual, it’s a wonder that sex is so popular. And it is popular, especially among vertebrate species. There are relatively few asexual species of vertebrates, and those that do appear are more extinction prone compared with sexual species. Experts explain the relative abundance of sexual vertebrate species as a result of the benefits of the greater genetic diversity they create. That genetic diversity provides more potential for adaptive change, especially in variable environments. Sexual reproduction also provides an opportunity to select against and remove harmful mutations present in the individual parental contributions. No such opportunity exists for asexuals.

Sexual recombination is not horizontal evolution in the same sense or on the same scale as hybridization, lineage joining, and horizontal gene transfer. However, it facilitates novel gene combinations among populations, and it differs strongly from the centralized asexual reproductive process in which progeny receive all their genes from a single parent. Evolution tends to favor asexual reproduction in groups of organisms having high rates of reproduction, like bacteria, where less is invested in individual progeny.

HORIZONTAL GENE TRANSFER

Horizontal gene transfer both into and among eukaryotes was thought at first to be absent, then rare, and now, as databases grow, merely uncommon. Horizontal gene transfer for eukaryotes tends to occur in its microscopic groups, where it is roughly 80 percent less common than in bacteria and archaea. Current estimates indicate that microbial eukaryotes have genomes comprising 0.04 to 6.49 percent genes that have experienced horizontal gene transfer, though these numbers are likely to change as more surveys are done.29 Researchers Julia Van Etten and Debashish Bhattacharya write that “horizontal gene transfer is a small but significant player in the evolution of microbial eukaryotes,” and they provide examples where horizontal gene transfer has facilitated gain of adaptive functions and, in some cases, underpinned major lifestyle transitions. This includes transitioning to life in extreme environments of hot springs and of glaciers, and in organisms moving from salt water to freshwater.30

But there has also been considerable horizontal gene transfer from bacteria into nonmicrobial eukaryotes. Various insects, mollusks, sea anemones, and fungi are able to protect themselves from bacterial invaders using adopted bacterial defense genes that destroy cell walls. Some insects, slime molds, and diatoms have picked up bacterial genes allowing them to synthesize vitamins and sequester iron. Select fungi, land plants, diatoms, and algae have received and incorporated bacterial genes helping them cope with extreme environmental toxicity and stress.31 Horizontal gene transfer among plants has been strongly implicated in the evolution of some parasitic plants, with parasitic species gaining genes from their hosts.32

Keep in mind that horizontal gene transfer is not easy to identify. It requires exacting care in both lab and computer analyses. There have been more than a few reports of horizontal gene transfer that have turned out to be false and due to contamination of samples in the lab or problems in analysis and the assembly of genomes. However, given time, science is self-correcting, errors are identified, and the extent of known horizontal gene transfer continues to grow.33

The events of horizontal evolution result in multiple lines connecting single species—or genes—in phylogenetic networks. What do we know about the shape and complexity of these networks? The power law distribution for network connections describes the situation in which a few nodes (individual species or genes) serve as hubs having most of the network connections, while most nodes have relatively few. Examples of power law distributions include much of the internet, many social networks, and spreaders of infectious disease.34 All of these show the presence of relatively few hubs serving more traffic than most other network participants. Power law distributions show a tendency for the richly connected to become even more so.

Much remains to be learned about the direction and connectedness of phylogenetic networks for different organisms. However, as databases for whole genomes from organisms grow, experts have identified a few broad trends across life’s diversity. Phylogenetic horizontal gene transfer networks across bacterial genomes appear to be hub dominated, with power law distributions. For example, analyses of horizontal gene transfer among the genomes of bacteria living in human digestive tracts all showed a power law distribution, often called scale free, with some bacterial genes being hubs in the horizontal gene transfer network relative to other genes that transferred less often, if at all. Similarly, a study of horizontal gene transfer among a large set of virus genomes showed relatively few genes as hubs and moving most often within the horizontal gene transfer network.35

LICHENS AS INNOVATION

Lichens deserve special mention. They are an emergent innovation in and of themselves, based on the activities of multiple species. You may know them as the flat, crinkly plantlike organisms hugging rocks and fallen trees, or the low and small, coral-like figures scattered across the tundra. But “plantlike” doesn’t do them justice.

Lichens are composites. Or, if you prefer, call them meta-organisms or even an ecosystem of species partners. A single lichen houses two or more different species living symbiotically and jointly creating the body of the lichen, known as the thallus. Usually, one species is fungal (the mycobiont) the other is either algal or bacterial (the photobiont). In some lichens, there are more than one contributing photobiont and/or mycobiont species. In addition to the primary symbionts, there are other species of fungi, algae, and nonphotosynthetic bacteria, all found within lichen thalli. These latter additional species are part of the lichen microbiome, the full set of its inhabiting microbes.36

The functions for the species composing this complex meta-organism, often called a holobiont, and the extent of their genetic integration are not precisely known. Many bacterial species residing within lichens are known to contribute to the nutrient supply for mycobionts, the production of hormones, and resistance to pathogens.37 However, many lichen symbionts retain the ability for independent living.

The overarching innovation of lichens is combining the lifestyles of their component species to become the most widely distributed eukaryotes on Earth, colonizing places neither algae nor fungi could on their own. Fungi gain sugars as food from algal photosynthesis, and algae gain shelter, protection from desiccation, and access to mineral nutrients from food digestion by fungi. But beyond the cold calculus of who does what for whom and why, the individuals combine their lives, simply because they can. Why not? Many lichen species thrive in the absence of soil and can tolerate extremes of heat and dehydration. Antarctic lichens taken into the lab readily survived a month’s exposure to a simulated Martian environment. Lichens make the case, on their own, for the astonishing power of symbioses and horizontal evolution to create new life-forms with new abilities and adaptations. That some lichen mycobionts have been found to hybridize adds to their compounding carnival of horizontal evolution.38

As we’ll see in the next chapter, the discovery of lichens as innovative composites with multiple species was initially dismissed as laughable. Early dismissal or neglect of greater significance beyond a small set of species was common for other mechanisms of horizontal evolution too. But the evidence supporting the new narrative was hiding in plain sight.

3 Seeds of Change

The past is never dead. It’s not even past.

—WILLIAM FAULKNER, REQUIEM FOR A NUN

Horizontal evolution had been hiding in plain sight for a long time. Hybridization of species, joining of lineages, and horizontal gene transfer have all been known to exist for a century or more. Their mechanisms and frequency across life-forms, however, were poorly understood for a long time, and they were not seen to be as consequential for life’s evolution as we view them now. The initial observations and discoveries were underappreciated, if not ridiculed and rejected outright, when first reported. In this chapter I trace the early history of ideas about horizontal evolution. I seek to show that a new, updated narrative for evolution is a matter of incorporating our current understanding as complementary to the traditional narrative, rather than being based on entirely novel mechanisms or ideas.

Hybridization among Species

Carl Linnaeus is well known for his efforts to name, describe, and classify all living species. This system, still in use, assigns genus and species epithets as unique two-word Latin names. With the help of a legion of globe-trotting students and dedicated correspondents, he was the system’s lead developer and popularizer. He classified all species into nested groups—first into genera then classes and orders—according to their similarities. Between 1735 and 1766 his book Systema naturae went through 12 editions. In those three decades, he classified around 9,000 species of plants and 4,400 species of animals. What had been a slim volume of 12 pages became a three-volume series of 2,400 pages. The 1758 tenth edition of Systema naturae is taken as the formal beginning of zoological nomenclature, and his 1753 publication of Species plantarum is the formal starting point for modern botanical nomenclature. His first love was botany, and he was an avid gardener, experimenting with breeding both local and exotic plants.

Linnaeus was born 98 years before Darwin, and professed fervent belief in God’s creation of species; however, his astute observations of plant variation in the wild and garden experiments in hybridizing plants led him to state in 1760: “It is impossible to doubt that there are new species produced by hybrid generation … [and] it appears to follow, that the many species of plants in the same genus in the beginning could not have been otherwise than one plant, and have arisen from this hybrid generation.”1 The twelfth edition of Linnaeus’s Systema naturae omitted a claim that he had made in earlier editions that new species do not form, implicitly allowing the possibility of recent speciation. So, although he was a creationist, he also understood that some new species had arisen since the creation based on natural processes.

But Linnaeus was not an evolutionist. He did not argue that all new species were the result of natural selection and adaption as Darwin did. Linnaeus suggested that new species arising via hybridization was simply a natural means for filling in the preexisting details of God’s plan. Still, hybridization among distinct species in the wild was not accepted as a mechanism for new species’ origins in Linnaeus’s time, nor in Darwin’s. Direct observations were lacking, and mechanisms to maintain hybrid distinctiveness, given likely competition and potential to backcross with parental forms, were not known. Instances of hybrids, like mules, being unable to reproduce also argued against it. Linnaeus’s claim for novel species having hybrid origins was largely discounted at the time. He was no stranger to disappointment. He sought, unsuccessfully, to bolster the Swedish economy with non-native plant crops like coffee, tea, and rice. This was before the limits to species’ geographic ranges and their reasons were known.

Darwin was well aware of hybridization among species of plants and of animals. He wrote a chapter on “hybridism” in his 1859 book, citing his own observations and extensive correspondence with others. The correspondence indicates that hybridization among species was common knowledge among many, especially naturalists and plant and animal breeders. Plant species discussed as engaging in hybridization within their groups included petunias, rhododendrons, willows, gourds, figworts, and tobacco plants. His discussion of animals hybridizing within their groups included deer, pheasants, moths, geese, dogs, and cattle.

Darwin’s main concern was in showing an “essential distinction between species and [their] varieties” such that crossbreeding of different species generally resulted in sterile progeny, whereas offspring from breeding among varieties of the same species were fertile. He struggled to understand why progeny from some interspecific crosses were sterile while others were not. He cautioned that fertility or sterility of progeny was not sufficient on its own for distinguishing species from their varieties.2 He did not write about hybridization among species as a significant force in life’s diversification.

In 1866, Austrian biologist, mathematician, and monk Gregor Mendel published a detailed series of hybridization experiments with garden pea plants in the proceedings of the Brünn Natural History Society. He showed that information is passed from parents to progeny in discrete units (later called genes) that exist in pairs (later called alleles), one from each parent. He coined the terms “dominant” and “recessive” to describe whether an allele is expressed or masked in progeny. His experiments broke through the conceptual impediment of blending inheritance for evolution, in which all traits of progeny are intermediate to those of the parents. If blending inheritance were true, variation among individuals in a species would be homogenized, leaving no variation for the process of natural selection. Few read his publication when it first appeared.

However, after the publication’s rediscovery in 1900, its findings provided a framework for what grew to become modern genetics, including an important role for hybridization studies in learning about evolution and the heritability of traits. At that time, there was simply no way to measure the impact of hybridization in generating the diversity of species across life-forms. Attention was focused on other issues, such as whether and how Mendel’s findings conflicted with Darwin’s views on species origins.3

We now have the ability to evaluate the frequency of past hybridization events by comparing entire genomes across many species. Researchers are finding hybridization among species to be much more common than previously thought, in both plants and animals. Recent estimates are that roughly 25 percent of the world’s flowering plant species and 10 percent of animals have arisen through hybridization.4 Though hybrid origins are less common in animals, recent discoveries include examples in insects, cichlid fishes, and birds, one being a species of Darwin’s finch from the Galapagos Islands.

Furthering the narrative of widespread integration among species, hybridization has knock-on effects, known as introgression, that continue the genetic mixing among species even if a new species does not arise. This is seen when hybrid individuals keep their ability to reproduce with one or both parent species, or with closely related species. Hybrid introgression has been found in all sexually reproducing species radiations (see table 1.1) evaluated to date with genome-scale data, including studies of stickleback fishes, Heliconius butterflies, and Anolis lizards. Many other examples of introgression are known, such as between coyotes and gray wolves and between polar and brown bears. Genetic mixing for the two bears dates back to the last glacial maximum about 20,000 years ago, when polar and brown bears overlapped in range more broadly. Estimates for the frequency of introgression are certain to increase as methods for revealing it improve.5

All hybridization phenomena, including introgression, qualify as horizontal evolution, because genetic material is exchanged between different species, rather than between parents and offspring, the path of vertical evolution. They denote networking rather than branching.

Recombination and Horizontal Gene Transfer

The hybridization discussion above applies to sexually reproducing plants, fungi, and animals. However, as noted in chapter 2, recombination among bacterial lineages accomplishes genetic mixing analogous to hybridization. The three primary mechanisms (transformation, transduction, conjugation) are also forms of horizontal gene transfer, and for this reason the early history for recombination and horizontal gene transfer is shared.

The beginning of their shared history is tightly linked with one of the most consequential biological discoveries of any era, the discovery that DNA is the material of inheritance. We can begin in 1928 when British microbiologist Frederick Griffith (figure 3.1) published results of an exciting set of experiments. Using mice and Streptococcus pneumoniae, the bacterium causing pneumonia, he demonstrated the ability of a nonvirulent bacterial strain to be “transformed” into a virulent one. He did this by mixing the two strains together in mice. Prior to mixing, the virulent strain was killed by boiling, yet it still managed to transform the living benign strain into a living virulent one. Griffith died in his laboratory during a German air attack on London in 1941. That same year, Oswald Avery, a researcher at the Rockefeller Institute in New York City, picked up on Griffith’s efforts to identify the transforming substance in the killed virulent strain of Streptococcus.

Avery and two colleagues, Colin MacLeod and Maclyn McCarty, went on to demonstrate and publish in 1944 that DNA was the mysterious transforming factor, and more broadly, DNA was the material of inheritance. Avery wrote to his brother, hinting at the horizontal nature of the transformation, that “nucleic acids [DNA] … induce predictable and hereditary changes in cells. Sounds like a virus—may be a gene.”6 But bacterial transformation, with direct uptake of genetic material by one bacterium from another, would not be appreciated as a form of horizontal gene transfer until decades later. Perhaps the role of DNA in vertical, parent-to-progeny inheritance had to be understood first. Nonetheless, the earliest evidence of horizontal gene transfer in bacteria was published and considered well before its evolutionary consequences for other microbes and other life-forms were understood.

Another mechanism for horizontal gene transfer was discovered in 1951 when microbiologist Victor Freeman at the University of Washington in Seattle found that he could use a virus to infect and carry genes that changed a nonvirulent strain of bacteria to virulent one. The bacterium involved, Corynebacterium diphtheriae, was the cause of diphtheria, and the intention of the study was to learn about possible ways to manage contagious disease. Similarly, in 1952, Zinder and Joshua Lederberg working at the University of Wisconsin found evidence for genetic exchange among Salmonella bacteria, which can cause stomach flu, mediated by viruses, and coined the term “transduction” for it. These authors realized they were looking at a mechanism of inheritance that differed from the vertical, parent-to-progeny, mode. The frequency and speed of lateral gene transfer for bacterial evolution was not recognized until the 1950s, with the rapid and global spread of bacterial resistance to antibiotics. Still, the role of horizontal gene transfer in the evolution of life would remain poorly known until many more species and their genomes could be explored.

Joining of Lineages

Simon Schwendener was on to something (figure 3.2). And he had no way of knowing how big. He knew simply that what he saw when looking at lichens through his microscope in the 1860s defied the experts. As far as they were concerned lichens were plants, and one lichen was one plant. Instead, the 28-year-old Swiss botanist saw algae and confusing masses of threadlike filaments from a microscopic fungus. This made no sense if lichens were plants. Over months and years of painstaking observation with equipment we’d now deem primitive, he watched as threadlike fungal filaments called hyphae developed independently from the algae that were clustered along the lichen surfaces. He watched as hyphae first surrounded the algae and then stimulated them to reproduce. He worked tirelessly to enhance the power of his light microscope and repeat his experiments in order to dispel his own initial doubts about his observations. Eventually, he became certain his lichens resulted from the interactions between two different life-forms, algae and fungi, and that no plants were involved. Not only was he defying the plant experts, he contradicted the conventional view of species origins, as explained by Darwin, in which new species evolved by splitting from others, not by joining them.

Schwendener presented his findings to the Swiss Naturalists Club in 1867, saying that lichens are colonies, composed of thousands of individuals of algae and one or more controlling fungus. He compared the fungal and algal components to spider and prey, respectively, with the fungus stimulating the algae trapped in its net of filaments rather than feeding on them. His audience was deeply skeptical. Some experts said Schwendener’s imagination was overactive. Others just called him a simpleton. No one could produce lichens by combining algae and fungi.

Andreas Schimper (figure 3.3), another young botanist, was also compelled to question orthodoxy. Peering through his microscope in 1882, the 27-year-old watched an array of tiny green spheres reproduce as they floated within a single isolated plant cell. One sphere become two, over and over. He coined their name “chloroplastiden,” “chloroplasts” in English, for their green pigment, chlorophyll. Schimper puzzled over the evolution of such complexity, noting that the chloroplasts might be mistaken for organisms in their own right, if not for their confinement within plant cells. He published a manuscript in 1883 pointing out the strong resemblance between dividing chloroplasts and free-living, dividing cyanobacteria. Schimper casually stated in a footnote that if plant reproductive cells contained chloroplasts, that would support the preposterous idea that green plants originated as a symbiotic relationship between chloroplasts and some other organism. Though a few other botanists noticed the same similarity between chloroplasts and some bacteria, his speculation was ignored. No symbioses (species living together) among plants and other organisms were known that might lend the idea a bit of plausibility. Schimper did not pursue the idea either, and carried on with his studies of chloroplasts and plant ecology.

Konstantin Mereschkowski, a Russian biologist, did pick up on Schimper’s wild idea about 20 years later. He gathered evidence supporting a much more detailed scenario for the evolution of complex cells, in which both chloroplasts and mitochondria arose via symbiotic relationships, in publications between 1905 and 1918. He was well aware of the power of symbioses, being a student of lichens as well as hydras, small freshwater invertebrates that often live together with algae. Unlike Schwendener and Schimper, Mereschkowski explicitly rejected the Darwinian notion that natural selection could explain all or even most biological novelty, and this predisposed him to seeing the evolution of cells as a process of symbioses instead of selection on small, incremental variations among individual organisms.

But the idea of symbiotic origins for chloroplasts and mitochondria within cells was in direct conflict with the accepted mid-twentieth-century view that chromosomes in cell nuclei were the primary if not sole source of heritable materials for organisms. The view that chloroplasts and mitochondria evolved gradually from materials already within cell nuclei was a much better fit with Darwinian natural selection as catalyzing evolutionary change. Evolution was generally not viewed as advancing by symbioses as outlined by Schwendener, Schimper, and Mereschkowski. Mereschkowski in particular felt himself marginalized by the scientific community. He was a deeply troubled person and eventually took his own life.

Today we can look back and see that key features of the lineage-joining phenomena investigated and described by Schwendener, Schimper, Mereschkowski, Griffith, Linnaeus, and many other pioneers have been not only confirmed but greatly expanded. After Schwendener’s presentation at the Swiss Naturalists Club in 1867, scientists agreed that compelling proof of lichens’ dual nature could be provided if lichens could be produced experimentally from mixtures of fungi and algae in the lab. After many failures, a group led by German botanist Christian Ernst Stahl in 1877 found the right conditions and was able to grow lichens from fungal and algal components. Fast-forwarding to the present, some lichens are now known to include a dozen or more partner species of algae and fungi. The dual nature of lichen evolution, first glimpsed in the mid-1800s, has now expanded to house a veritable community of species.

Obligate symbioses among formerly independent species have been discovered across many life-forms. Invertebrate animals such as corals and jellyfish are reliant on algal symbionts. Symbioses with nitrogen-fixing bacteria are common within the roots of many plant species and key for the plant’s survival. Fungal structures called mycorrhiza stem from symbiotic relationships between fungi and plants, and they increase the ability of plant roots to absorb soil nutrients. These symbioses have been found in over 90 percent of all plant families examined, including some fossil plants about 400 million years old. Symbioses are frequently seen between insects and bacteria that assist in digestion of food and defense against pathogens. In discoveries that will surprise many, our own immune systems, as well as those of all multicellular organisms, were shaped by symbioses with viruses. Symbioses take many forms, ranging from obligate to occasional, and with or without genetic integration. Their evolutionary consequences, however, are deep and widespread across species.

Early hypotheses by Schimper in 1883 and Mereschkowski in 1905 on origins of chloroplasts were not well supported by evidence until the 1970s. Even then, evolution was rarely viewed as advancing by cooperation. Endosymbioses as an explanation for the evolution of complex cells remained on the fringes for most of the twentieth century. American biologist Lynn Margulis was key in the effort to revive and extend these ideas (figure 3.4). In 1967, following rejection from numerous journals, she published a paper (as Lynn Sagan) in the Journal of Theoretical Biology. This paper presented a bold synthesis of old and new ideas about the microbial origins of chloroplasts and mitochondria. She argued for their microbial origins as an idea that explained key aspects of a major evolutionary transition, from single-celled organisms like bacteria, lacking internal structures, to more complex organisms, like plants and animals, whose numerous cells did have vital, internal structures.7

Margulis noted that as photosynthesizing organisms, specifically cyanobacteria, added oxygen to the environment, ancient anaerobic life-forms would be unable to survive, unless they adapted somehow. They needed a way to metabolize oxygen, and this could be gained by adopting oxygen-metabolizing bacteria, which could become mitochondria. Similarly, early life-forms could benefit from taking in photosynthesizing cyanobacteria, which became chloroplasts, as a means for producing food.

Margulis’s ideas were deeply influenced by Hans Ris, a Swiss-born cell biologist and her graduate-school mentor at the University of Wisconsin. She argued persuasively and persistently, reviving the largely forgotten views of Schimper, Mereschkowski, and others. And her timing was right. She was able to marshal new supporting evidence from electron microscopy showing more structural similarities between microbes and organelles, from biochemistry showing that organelles have their own DNA, and from paleontology showing the oldest known life-forms to be single-celled bacteria predating the origin of cells with organelles. She rode the rising tide of support for the microbial origins of chloroplasts and mitochondria, including clear genetic evidence of relatedness from groundbreaking analyses by American microbiologist Carl Woese. She relished the fight, demonstrating that aspects of the current evolutionary view were wrong and in need of correction. Cooperation and symbioses could be more powerful than competition and selection acting on minor genetic variation. Evolutionary change could happen suddenly. Much of the pattern of life’s history was networked, and unlike a tree whose branches only split. In her words, “Life did not take over the globe by combat, but by networking.”8

Lamarck

Jean-Baptiste Lamarck (1744–1829) was a prolific and influential French zoologist. Although his views and Darwin’s differed markedly, Lamarck preceded Darwin by about 50 years in publishing a theory of life’s natural evolution and change over time. Lamarck’s concept of evolution lacked the compelling mechanism of natural selection that Darwin developed and used so effectively to convince his peers of evolution’s reality. Lamarck’s name is linked, oddly enough, to a concept that was taken as common knowledge by many of his predecessors. Initially, “Lamarckism” or “Lamarckian inheritance” stood for the inheritance in progeny of traits acquired by their parents based on the use or disuse of body parts. In Lamarck’s words, “the law of nature by which new individuals receive all that has been acquired in organization [of bodies] during the lifetime of their parents … [is] so much attested by the facts, that there is no observer who has been unable to convince himself of its reality.” He emphasized the role of environment and behavior in the shaping of animal bodies and parts over time, and was the first to associate inheritance of acquired characters with “the causes that have led to the astonishing diversity of animals.”9 For Lamarck, environments induce behaviors that can cause heritable change in bodies. Even Darwin supported the idea that use and disuse of bodily features in behavior could be influential in evolution.

Lamarckian inheritance began losing support with the advent of Mendelian inheritance and experiments on heritability in plants in the early 1900s. And it was largely abandoned in its original formulation by biologists in the twentieth century. However, as new methods and molecular tools have been developed, the evidence for some inheritance of acquired characters has grown. For example, acquired changes in the chemical environment of a developing embryo can influence its gene expression and, in turn, body form. This is part of epigenetics, a field of study dedicated to understanding the influence of environment and behavior on how genes work. The important distinction here is that change in gene expression differs from change in the DNA sequence of genes themselves. Other examples of new evidence for inheritance of acquired traits come from the study of immunity to disease. For example, some bacteria are able to insert a piece of foreign DNA into their own genome, resulting in acquired, heritable resistance to disease infection. Importantly, the foreign DNA scavenged by the bacteria is from their environment.10

The mechanisms of direct horizontal evolution—namely, species hybridization, joining, and horizontal gene transfer—also result in the heritability of acquired characteristics. They are rarely considered in this light, but they can or should be, because that is the reality. Individuals acquiring genes by horizontal evolution pass them on to their progeny. Lamarckian inheritance has long been viewed and taught as a historical error. Lamarck’s own descriptions were, unsurprisingly, vague and lacking detailed evidence of mechanism. He was writing prior to the insights of Darwin, Alfred Russel Wallace, Mendel, and others, and he knew nothing about DNA as the material of inheritance. As a result, the terms “Lamarckism” and “Lamarckian inheritance” have a strong negative connotation, and most experts avoid the terms as a descriptor for horizontal evolution. The literal inheritance of acquired traits, however, does happen. It is consistent with horizontal evolution, and complementary to heritability of parental traits, as in conventional vertical evolution. My effort here to update evolution’s overarching narrative with a greater and crucial role for horizontal evolution also means recognizing a greater and crucial role for the inheritance of acquired traits in life’s evolution, regardless of whether or not one uses the outdated descriptor “Lamarckian.”

Humans and Horizontal Evolution

Let’s conclude this historical chapter by making things personal, considering a few of the many horizontal evolution events in our own past. We’ll see these events have been key in aspects of our digestion, origins, reproduction, and breathing. Horizontal evolution is a key element in our human story.

Lots of people love sushi. But some are better equipped than others to digest it because of horizontal evolution. The code for a digestive enzyme has been transferred, via horizontal gene transfer, from marine bacteria to human gut bacteria in Japanese human populations. Sushi’s dark seaweed wrapping, nori, is difficult if not impossible for most people to digest, as its carbohydrates include hardened molecules not found in land plants. This story was uncovered when scientists studying enzymes in marine bacteria that are able to digest nori found one of the same enzymes in gut bacteria of Japanese people but absent from gut bacteria of North Americans.11 The marine bacteria were likely ingested along with the nori, enabling the horizontal gene transfer and the digestive benefits for humans that followed. But the full story of horizontal evolution and human digestion is bigger still. Most of the necessary carbohydrate-digesting enzymes in our guts are provided by bacteria. Relatively few are encoded directly in human genomes, feeding the digestive collaboration.

Moving on, there’s nothing pure about recent human history, genetically speaking. Modern humans (Homo sapiens) had been hybridizing on occasion with some of our closest relatives up to the time of their disappearance from the fossil record (figure 3.5). Genetic data acquired from the remains of now-extinct organisms suggests that our lineage was interbreeding as recently as about 60,000 years ago with Neanderthals (H. neanderthalensis) and 30,000 years ago with Denisovans (H. denisova). Whether the latter two distinctive groups are better designated as subspecies of modern humans or as separate species is debatable, but the point about interbreeding and horizontal evolution among distinctive groups remains. This interbreeding has apparently provided us with some beneficial traits. Mutations providing adaptability to high altitudes have been traced to Siberian Denisovans, and mutations strengthening keratin proteins, improving skin and hair durability, come from Neanderthals. Current genomic studies attest that an earlier, conventional view of modern human races as the result of each group branching off neatly from a single ancestral population following migration out of Africa is simplistic and mistaken. Rather, modern races are an amalgamation of groups whose individuals have a long history of geographic movement and occasional interbreeding.12

Pregnancy and live birth are essential human experiences. They are also essentially the result of horizontal evolution. Their evolution required some neutralizing of the mother’s immune system, which would naturally attack the embryo as an antigen, a set of foreign cells different from her own. This neutralizing of the immune system has been and is still being wrought by a mind-boggling set of relationships between placental mammals, including humans, and retroviruses. Roughly 46 percent of the three billion bases in the human genome are derived from prior infection with retroviruses. This includes the human endogenous retroviral sequences known as HERVs. We are still learning about their varied functions, but current evidence indicates their vital roles in mediating mothers’ immune system response to the fetus and in development of the placenta. Beyond the workings of internal gestation for mammals, experiments with human cells also show a role for HERVs in the growth of neurons and maintenance of their functions in the brain.13 Our debt to HERVs and their horizontal gene transfer may be deeper still.

We’re hooked on oxygen. It can be fatal, but we’re able to successfully metabolize it as a result of ancient horizontal evolution. The key event, as noted earlier, was an endosymbiotic relationship, let’s call it a swallowing, of a bacterium, by an archaean about 1.8 billion years ago. The bacterium survived, reproduced, and over time its progeny accommodated to life inside an archaean. Ultimately, the bacterium gave rise to mitochondria. The swallowed bacteria had previously evolved an ability to transform oxygen and sugar into energy, in form of ATP (adenosine triphosphate). Its archaea host, lacking that ability, was a beneficiary. The two lineages, in combination with many others, gave rise to eukaryotes. This early species-joining event is not merely part of our human evolution, it is vital to our existence.

We are learning the hard way that infectious disease will always be with us. Population growth, frequent interaction with microbes, and global travel set us up for their rapid spread. Especially so when the pathogens are airborne. Our immune systems, though stunning in complexity and speed of response, can be overwhelmed. Why? Because microbial pathogens can be equally fast in their ability to evolve and elude our immune defense. The viruses causing human influenza routinely swap gene segments among themselves. This is a kind of recombination and horizontal evolution that allows them to elude the immune defense and vaccines developed against prior forms just a few months earlier. This is why influenza is a perennial problem. The SARS-CoV-2 coronavirus causing COVID-19 shows a different, less frequent kind of recombination and horizontal evolution. But these viruses’ recombination, combined with the rapid mutation of genes, means the target for immunity can shift quickly.

Finally, channel your ancient naturalist’s instincts and consider the bird watchers’ connection with horizontal evolution. Birding, done well, forces your focus. Tune out human noise. Search the skies, listen for songs beneath the wind, associate species with terrain, and feel the changing seasons. Hybridization among bird species, typically closest relatives, is hard to detect, but it is much more common than previously thought. Estimates for the percentage of bird species that hybridize have risen, based on improving data and methods, from 9.2 percent in 1992, to 16 percent in 2015.14 The latter is surely an underestimate as molecular analyses of hybridization, with greater discriminating power than even dedicated birders, are only beginning. Hybridizing species are known from all the major bird groups, excluding those with relatively few species members, from albatrosses to owls to hummingbirds. Hybridizing species include various Darwin’s finches from the Galapagos Islands, birds-of-paradise from Papua New Guinea and Australia, and blue-winged and golden-winged warblers in North and Central America, to name just a few.15 This has significance for conservation efforts, as some species may be lost by means of hybridizing. Though our current understanding of horizontal evolution is much improved, there is still more hiding in plain sight that we’ve not yet found.

4 Life’s Edge

The entire course of evolution … is to a large extent shaped by the coevolution of these [all] organisms with genetic parasites including viruses, transposons, and more.

—EUGENE KOONIN, THE LOGIC OF CHANCE

The conventional narrative for life’s evolution is largely silent on the topics of viruses and life’s origins. The silence tells us the conventional narrative is dated. Ever since their discovery in the late nineteenth century, viruses have been excluded from the living. As poorly understood, obligate parasites with minimal structure beyond their small set of DNAs or RNAs, they have been easy to exclude from the living and thus from evolution’s narrative. Viruses were merely rogue chemical specks with no meaningful history and few genes. But as our knowledge grows regarding viruses’ antiquity, complex life cycles, and actions, that is changing.

Similarly, the narrative for life’s evolution begins once life has already emerged from nonlife. Transitioning from a world of chemicals to one with living organisms has also been left outside the narrative. And understandably so. The earliest stages are not biological enough to include in the narrative, and, more to the point, many questions about the means and history of the transition remain unresolved. But here, too, our understanding is growing.

It is not a coincidence that both topics—origins of viruses and of origins of all life—have been absent from the conventional narrative. Knowledge on these topics has been and is still hard to come by, given the extreme age of their beginnings and ongoing uncertainty about Earth’s early environments. But progress is being made, and, it turns out, the topics seem closely linked. Some of the current ideas about viruses and life’s origins to be discussed in this chapter remain speculative and hypotheses for testing. However, current evidence supports their serious consideration, and I include them in evolution’s new narrative. They are fundamental features of life’s history and too important to ignore. Viruses have been powerful agents of integration among life-forms throughout life’s evolution, including life’s origins. I also explore the fuzzy boundaries between life and nonlife, and the concept of the holobiont as an individual including a host and its symbionts.

What Is Life?

There is no consensus for defining life. There are no physical components or substances whose presence or absence neatly distinguishes life from nonlife. Surprising? Our lived experience would seem to make us experts. We make snap decisions all day long about what is alive and what is not. Push the door not the person by the door. Step on gravel not the snake. But so much of learning is about getting over ourselves and recognizing the limits of our experience. Most life-forms are invisible, intangible, silent, and odorless to us. We don’t know what naturally lives on our skin or inside our bodies unless we peer through a microscope or read about it and trust the source. Nor do we know what we share with other life but not with nonlife. Sure, reproduction and genetic inheritance are common to all life, but they are not unique to it, being present in a variety of nonliving entities such as plasmids, transposons, chloroplasts, and each of your individual cells. Defining life changes from easy to daunting as one focuses on the boundary cases.

There is, however, broad recognition that life is a process, a system that evolves over time (box 4.1). Biologists tend to focus on organismal processes and systems such as metabolism, growth, heredity, reproduction, natural selection, and evolution in describing life. Whereas chemists and physicists often take the more general perspective of self-sustaining chemical systems. These views are compatible when we reduce organisms to their constituent chemical elements. Hopes and dreams aside, you and I are 99 percent oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, carbon, calcium, and phosphorus, with trace amounts of sulfur, potassium, sodium, chlorine, and magnesium. We would be as stones if not for the astonishing, self-sustaining processes and systems of life.

Viewing life as comprising process and systems rather than the contributing physical entities has not yet become mainstream. But it is getting there. Species and organisms are ephemeral, whereas processes persist over time.1 Processes and systems are more fundamental, and are uniquely shared across all life’s diversity, unlike any particular physical trait or substance. Organisms themselves comprise process and systems, from developmental to metabolic and reproductive, and they are best understood in that context. For example, understanding an individual frog requires understanding its life-cycle changes, from developing embryo to tadpole to adult frog. Understanding the adult alone is an incomplete view of the organism. This perspective has become important in understanding viruses, as described below.

Box 4.1. Defining Life as Systems and Process

• Life is the property of a system that continuously draws negative entropy (maintains orderliness), and delays decay into thermodynamic equilibrium (Schrödinger)

• Life is an expected, collectively self-organized property of catalytic polymers (Kauffman)

• Life is a property of any population of entities possessing those properties that are needed if the population is to evolve by natural selection (Maynard Smith and Szathmáry)

• Life is a process characterized by evolution, natural selection, genetic inheritance, complexity, organization, indeterminacy, variability, and uniqueness (Mayr)

• Life is a self-sustained chemical system capable of Darwinian evolution (NASA Astrobiology Institute; Joyce et al.)

• Life, whatever else it may be, is certainly a regularity among material processes (Eigen)

Sources: Schrödinger, E., 1944, What Is Life?, Cambridge: Cambridge University Press; Mayr, E., 1982, The Growth of Biological Thought: Diversity, Evolution, and Inheritance, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press (statement is an approximation of pp. 51–67); Kauffman, S. A., 1993, The Origins of Order: Self-Organization and Selection in Evolution, Oxford: Oxford University Press; Joyce, G. F., Deamer D.W., and Fleischaker, G., 1994, Origins of Life: The Central Concepts, Boston, MA: Jones and Bartlett; Maynard Smith, J. M., and Szathmary, E., 2000, The Origins of Life: From the Birth of Life to the Origin of Language, Oxford: Oxford University Press; Eigen, M., 2013, From Strange Simplicity to Complex Familiarity, Oxford: Oxford University Press.

All life, as we know it, is centered around DNA or RNA molecules that copy or replicate themselves. These are replicating carriers of genetic information. The full set of such replicators includes all the familiar life-forms of eukaryotes (like us), archaea, and bacteria. It also, however, includes a series of mysterious-sounding entities called mobile genetic elements (MGEs). MGEs typically encode proteins that mediate their own movement, and that of other DNAs, within a genome or between genomes of different individuals and even different species. MGEs have various degrees of autonomy to replicate and reproduce on their own. This includes variation in the ability to produce the nucleotides and amino acids (the resources) for replication and assembly of their progeny. Thus, not all replicators can reproduce on their own, and those that can’t are considered nonliving and parasitic.

Evolutionists Koonin and Starokadomskyy have described a paradigm to understand and organize the diversity of these replicating genetic elements.2 Box 4.2 describes various replicators and MGEs, and figure 4.1 shows variation in two measures of their autonomy. The vertical axis indicates their relative ability to produce the resources of nucleotides and amino acids for replication and assembly of progeny. The horizontal axis indicates the MGEs’ autonomy of movement based on the presence or absence of vehicles, and, if present, whether the vehicle is a virus or a cell. Vehicles facilitate acquisition of resources and dispersal of progeny. Organelles include mitochondria and chloroplasts.

Our increasing knowledge of MGEs and their work as agents of horizontal evolution, moving DNAs among individual and species of organisms, indicates their role in evolution’s new narrative emphasizing integration. For example, the genomes of many individual organisms are known to have fewer genes than their populations as a whole. That is, not all of the genes found within a species or microbial strain are found in all individuals. The full set of genes for a species or strain is known as its pan-genome, and the subset of genes shared by all individuals is the core genome. The larger size and greater inclusivity of the pan-genome stems, in part, from the fact that MGEs operate differently across individuals, resulting in differential gain or loss of genes. Early studies of pan-genomes necessarily focused on bacteria and viruses with relatively small genomes. Recent work, however, is showing existence of pan-genomes among eukaryotes too. Table 4.1 shows the known range of core genome size (as a percentage of the pan-genome) across groups. For example, the core genome of the common gut bacterium of humans and other mammals Escherichia coli has a core genome that includes only 8 percent of the pan-genome. In bacteria generally, core genes are essential to the life of the organism, while noncore genes are often associated with virulence, defense response, and communication.3

Box 4.2. Diversity of Replicators: Mobile Genetic Elements and Their Key Features

Genetic Element

Comment

An infectious RNA molecule that encodes no protein but recruits some from the host, causing disease in plants

Transposable element (transposon)

A segment of DNA that is capable of moving into a new position within the same or another chromosome or plasmid

Most encode enzymes, such as recombinases, transposases, and integrases, that actively mediate transposition

Group I intron

Large self-splicing ribozyme that catalyzes its own excision from mRNA, tRNA, or rRNA precursors

Most group I introns encode endonucleases that mediate the intron transposition and additionally facilitate splicing; may be ancestral to viroids

Group II intron
