Departures from 100% for core genome size are a result of horizontal gene transfer, MGE activity, and the gain/loss of genes.

There are three key points to be drawn from this information. First, The relationship between core genome and pan-genome is influenced by MGE activities, especially replicators with reduced autonomy, such as transposons and viruses (figure 4.2). These MGE activities complement evidence presented in chapter 2 on the frequency and functional significance of horizontal gene transfer, contributing to the genetic integration among life-forms, as emphasized in evolution’s new narrative.

Second, there is a continuum for replicators in their degree of autonomy for those two measures (the ability to produce resources for replication, and their association with vehicles for movement). The continuum ranges from full autonomy for what we recognize as living organisms to no autonomy for plasmids and retroelements. Between those replicator extremes are different kinds of viruses. This continuum is consistent with the idea of life having fuzzy, inexact boundaries. This, in turn, is consistent with life as a dynamic, evolutionary process. We expect to see entities having variable degrees of autonomy as part of the processes of the origins of life from nonlife. The unlikely alternative is life’s complex processes being fully formed, or nearly so, from the beginning. There is also a network of evolutionary relationships between replicators without vehicles, such as plasmids, and viruses, based on their genes. This indicates that transitions from one type of replicator to another could have and likely did occur on multiple occasions over the long course of evolution.4 I’ll address this further below.

Third, the existence of such replicators as nonliving genetic parasites able to move within and among individuals appears to be inevitable in evolving systems. Parasitic replicators are found in all known life-forms, and they have been found to arise repeatedly and to persist in computer simulations of life’s evolution.5 This suggests that parasitic replicators and their capacity to move genetic materials horizontally have been an integral part of life’s evolution from the beginning, or close to it, over 3.8 billion years ago.

What Are Viruses?

Viruses are also difficult to define, though we all experience them as infectious agents that are too small to see.6 When you conjure in your mind an image of a spiky blob encasing some genes, what you have come up with is a virion. That is just one stage in the larger life cycle of a virus. Collectively, virus species present an enormous diversity of systems and lifestyles. A general virus life cycle includes stages for attachment to cells, entry to cells, removal of the virion’s protein coat, potential insertion into the host’s genome, replication and expression of genes, assembly of progeny, and release and dispersal of progeny by air or water or by lying dormant on surfaces to be taken up inadvertently by new hosts. Within hosts, viruses may seek particular tissue types whose cells they are uniquely adapted to infect.

There are many steps within each of these stages, of course, and their timing varies across viruses and hosts. SARS-CoV-2 may complete most of these steps within just one or two days, before onset of COVID-19 symptoms. HIV, causing AIDS, can remain integrated into the DNA of host cells for 15 years or longer before reactivation and production of offspring. Observing the integrated HIV genes during their dormancy alone misses their full life cycle process, just as watching an adult frog misses theirs. During their intracellular stage, viruses cleverly co-opt the host’s cellular machinery and genes for combined use together with the virus’s genes to produce thousands upon thousands of virus progeny.

Box 4.3. About Earth’s Viruses

• All cellular life-forms host distinct repertoires of viruses and other mobile genetic elements

• Viruses contribute continuously to the emergence of new genes in cellular life-forms by exchange of genetic material between cells and viruses

• Total number of viral particles: ~1032 (10× greater than the number of cells of all organisms)

• Virus particles in 1 cm3 of seawater: ~ 108

• Virus particles in 1 m3 of air: ~ 2 × 106 to 40 × 106

• Rate of viral infections in the oceans: ~ 1 × 1023/second

Viruses aren’t just ubiquitous, they are superabundant (box 4.3). Scientists are still in the early stages of evaluating their roles in ecosystems and in life’s evolution. There are seven different genome types among viruses, controlling their strategies for replication and expression. This observation led to the recognition of seven different virus classes (three with DNA and four with RNA) in 1971, known as the Baltimore classification. However, large molecular datasets for comparison among the main virus groups have only recently become available, and they show that the Baltimore classification does not accurately track virus evolution. Instead, analyses show viruses to have a complex network of relationships that defies representation with simple treelike lines of descent. Sound familiar? Both horizontal and vertical evolution of viral genes are abundant, transgressing virus class boundaries, roughly similar to the situation discussed in chapter 2 for the genes of bacteria and archaea.

A set of functionally important and relatively slow-to-change genes (hallmark genes) show that viruses from five Baltimore classes evolved on multiple independent occasions. That is, there are multiple unique origins for viruses. Further, analyses showed that one of the Baltimore classes—the single-stranded DNA viruses—includes independently evolved groups resulting from multiple independent instances of recombination between viruses and nonviral entities called plasmids (see box 4.2). Overall, the primary mechanisms for the network of relationships and horizontal evolution among viruses are recombination and horizontal gene transfer.

The rapid genetic evolution, both vertical and horizontal, overwrites and obscures the record of evolutionary relationships among many virus groups. However, enough of the molecular record is preserved, especially for the viral hallmark genes, to “harken back to the primordial pool of genetic elements. In particular, primordial RNA replicons (self-replicating molecules), including reverse-transcribing replicons akin to extant group II introns.”7 This provides the link to life’s origins to be discussed below.

Viruses have long inhabited a gray zone in our collective consciousness, spanning the animate and inanimate worlds. At first, they seemed impossibly small and disembodied, and the details of their natural history and vast diversity were unknown. As viruses became better understood, they remained outsiders, excluded from life for being obligate parasites, unable to reproduce on their own. But the barriers to their acceptance are fast disappearing, as the same features used to exclude viruses are now known for bacterial species as well. For example, multiple virus species, including Pithovirus sibericum, which is approximately half the width of a silk strand from a spider web, have individuals that are larger than many bacteria, and obligate parasitism among bacteria is now well known, including pathogens in the genera Rickettsia and Chlamydia.

But our knowledge of their complex life cycle stages and suite of activities is growing. And the more we learn, the more alive virus systems appear. Accepting the view outlined above that life comprises self-sustaining systems and processes, viruses would qualify as living and as organisms.8 Regardless of their ultimate consensus status as living or not, the only way to understand their change and potential is in the context of biological evolution. They infect all life-forms and are longstanding participants in life’s evolution. If alive, as I view them, they are symbionts; if not they are molecular parasites with lifelike systems. Either way, viruses are consequential agents in life’s evolution, illustrating evolution’s new narrative with greater emphasis on horizontal evolution and integration among life-forms.

For a glimpse of virus participation as symbionts, consider the stage in which many viruses are able to merge their own genome with their host’s genome through a process called endogenization. The virus infects the host’s egg or sperm cells, integrates all or part of its genome into that of the host, and the endogenized virus genes can then be inherited vertically among hosts for millions of years. There is a growing list of such endogenized virus elements (EVEs), including endogenized retroviruses (ERVs), that figure prominently in the functional lives of their hosts. One of the most fascinating EVEs is found in a group of wasps that parasitize caterpillars. This EVE is descended from the nudivirus group and functions now as a necessary delivery vector for genes that help ensure successful parasitism by the wasps.9 Evolution of this particular EVE over 100 million years or so has contributed to the evolution of novel wasp species and their adaptations to different host species. Table 4.2 describes seven additional EVEs with adaptive functions in their insect hosts. One of them, the HERV retrovirus-derived syncytin gene that functions in formation of the human placenta, was mentioned in chapter 3. Other EVEs assist hosts in their immune response by activating genes that saturate the host’s cell entry receptors and blocking infection. The number of such EVEs with beneficial functions will grow as survey efforts increase among hosts and endogenous viruses.

L., and Kunte, K., 2017, “Adaptive Genetic Exchange: A Tangled History of Admixture and Evolutionary Innovation,” Trends in Ecology & Evolution, 32(8):601–11; Ryan, F., 2020, “Viral Symbiosis in the Origins and Evolution of Life with a Particular Focus on the Placental Mammals,” in Symbiosis: Cellular, Molecular, Medical and Evolutionary Aspects, edited by Kloc, M., 2–24, Cham, Switzerland: Springer.

Life Becoming

The fact that life arose from nonlife is not controversial among scientists. But the exact means and sequence of events remains to be worked out. The relevant chemical associations have not left a record, fossil or otherwise, for us to analyze, and uncertainty about Earth’s early environments, over four billion years ago, renders prediction about the environment’s role imprecise. There is, however, broad agreement that self-replicating molecules, including viruses, played an important part in life’s origins. This is because we can readily observe the phenomena in them of self-replication of RNA and DNA, and the self-assembly of molecules, including lipid membranes, showing feasibility of some basic steps in life’s origin or origins.

There is also growing awareness that some viruses and other mobile genetic elements (MGEs) are ancient, predating the most recent common ancestor (MRCA) for all life 3.8 billion years ago. This, in turn, supports the view that viruses and MGEs are integral to life’s origins and evolution. Relevant evidence includes the following. A set of polymerase genes that aid replication are found across the primary virus groups, but are not found in any conventional life-forms, despite comprehensive searching. This suggests some viruses may not have been obligate parasites in the past.

Viruses also harbor the bulk of Earth’s genetic diversity, as would be expected for Earth’s oldest life-forms, with abundant time to accumulate that diversity. Correspondingly, viruses and other MGEs are ubiquitous, being involved in symbiotic or parasitic relationships with all life-forms. Reported absence of viruses from any given organism or species is more likely to reflect insufficient searching than actual absence. Viruses are known to readily mediate horizontal gene transfer among nonviruses, and to have influenced the evolution of all or nearly all organisms and their genomes to some degree. Further, computer simulations of evolution among replicators routinely result in emergence of parasitic or selfish replicators like viruses and MGEs as an essential feature. From a game-theory perspective, parasite-free conditions for life’s evolution are unstable.10

Recognizing that some virus lineages are as old as or older than life, and that some have been coevolving with organismal life from its beginnings, the questions of life’s origins and of viruses’ origins become deeply intertwined. I say “virus lineages” here to accommodate the apparent descent of some viruses from early replicons, which lacked the typical virus protein coats, known as capsids. There are three general scenarios discussed most often by experts regarding early interactions among virus lineages and life. One supposes that virus lineages arose first, ultimately giving rise to cellular life-forms. A second view supposes that viruses are descendants of early parasitic cells whose genomes and size have been reduced. The third supposes that viruses evolved from escaped genes of early life-forms. Current evidence is strongest, in my view, for the scenario in which virus lineages arose first; however, the three views are not mutually exclusive.11

Figure 4.2 shows the viruses-arising-first scenario in more detail. This scenario begins at the bottom of the figure, progressing upward through stages characterized by (1) naturally occurring chemical chains of RNA in an RNA world; (2) origins of diverse self-replicating molecules (replicons) including virus lineages; (3) a world with both RNA and DNA chains, with DNAs providing greater stability; (4) frequent horizontal gene transfer among RNA and DNA replicons, including early viruses; (5) compartmentalization and early DNA-based cells; and (6) horizontal gene transfer and diversification among MGEs, viruses, and cellular life-forms, including DNA-based cellular life’s most recent common ancestor. The significance, for our purpose here, is that horizontal gene transfer and horizontal evolution have been at play all along in life’s evolution, including its formative beginnings. This introduces both horizontal and vertical evolution into the emerging portion of evolution’s new narrative regarding life’s beginnings and the role of viruses.

What Are Individuals and Holobionts?

Language is imperfect, but we have no better tool for communicating the details of nature. “The limits of my language mean the limits of my world,” is the way philosopher Ludwig Wittgenstein puts it.12 Many of the terms and units that we use to parse the living world are vague. If they once seemed precise, time and greater knowledge have may have made them less so. As seen above, deciding what is alive and what is not alive is now difficult. Similarly, the concept of species and whether a given group qualifies as a species are subject to intense debate. The idea of an individual is similarly more complicated than it first appears.

Identifying biological individuals is like looking for yourself in a room full of mirrors. You are everywhere. And in nature, individuals are everywhere and nested to a vanishing point. We can easily identify individual organisms, like a zebra; collectives of organisms, like families and herds; and individual parts of organisms, like organs, cells, genomes, genes, and nucleotides. The same entity can represent a whole from one perspective, and a part from another. A whole zebra has many body parts, while the same zebra is but one part of a herd.

Applying these shifting perspectives to ourselves, let’s admit we are not exactly who we think we are. Eukaryotes, including ourselves, arose through the union of two distinct lineages via endosymbiosis, and we have two genomes to prove it. Thus, each of us has a dual nature based on these genomes. Is one genome more crucial and dominant, somehow canceling the other and our dual nature? No, both are vital. The smaller size of our mitochondrial genome compared with our nuclear genome does not change its essential nature for survival. The realities of genealogy and function cannot be denied just because a genome is relatively small. In addition, each of us has a legion of bacteria, of many different species, residing in and on our bodies. Some of these are also vital to our health and survival. We are each an ongoing collaboration or cooperative of many individuals of many species. This makes us, materially and historically, a composite, and shows our individuality as a matter of perspective.

Similarly, the extensive genealogical networks that result from horizontal evolution, as detailed in previous chapters, show that individuality for lineages, species, and genomes is a simplification. You are an individual, yes, but that masks a teeming sea of genes whose individual evolutionary histories differ from one another in some way. This is not to say that biological individuals are not real. They are. It is to say that the boundaries are relative, reflecting one’s focus. Horizontal evolution—including endosymbioses, hybridization, recombination, and horizontal gene transfer—comprises mechanisms of entanglement for individuals.

Taking a process-and-systems-oriented view in defining individuals is useful here, as it was for defining life above. A biological individual can be defined as a functionally integrated system in which components share the system’s evolutionary fate, owing to natural selection and chance events.13 For example, the assemblage of species including a host and all those living in and on it is known as a holobiont.14 Recognition of holobionts is recognition of hosts and their microbes as integrated and a functional unit. You and all your microbes are a functional unit. Occasional dysfunction and resulting sickness are a demonstration of that. Coevolution and horizontal gene transfer are not uncommon among holobiont members. The individual organisms are still a key focus for research, but they can be considered as part of a larger whole, the holobiont. This is analogous to an ant colony comprising the individual ants, a team comprising its football players, or even a nation comprising its states.

For a fascinating example, consider one of the best known holobiont systems, the Hawaiian bobtail squid and its microbial partners (figure 4.3). This is a nocturnal, walnut-sized squid that preys on shrimp. The Hawaiian bobtail squid themselves are vulnerable to predators such as eels, seals, and fish, and they have an elaborate adaptation known as a light organ, embedded in their ink sac, that helps them avoid detection. The light organ is packed with bacteria belonging to a single species, Vibrio fischeri. Collectively, they emit light emulating diffuse star and moonlight when conditions are right. That light helps cancel the squid’s shadow and hide it from predators. But there is even more to this holobiont and integration.

Female Hawaiian bobtail squid have another set of symbioses, with partners of multiple bacterial species housed in a second organ known as the accessory nidamental gland (ANG). The bacteria in the ANG have been found to provide defense against pathogens during squid embryogenesis. The complex interactions among the squid and its bacterial symbionts housed in the light organ and ANG are all necessary parts of the living process for the squid and for at least some of the bacterial species involved.15 Other microbial species living in close association with the host but not housed in those two glands are also part of the holobiont. The Hawaiian bobtail squid holobiont is a more comprehensive and inclusive individual than the squid alone. The holobiont is also a better demonstration of evolution’s new narrative and integration among individuals. I include the discussion of holobionts here as they are also a recently emerging arena for evolution’s new narrative, like viruses and life’s beginnings, including horizontal and vertical evolution.

This discussion of holobionts and individuals as functional units is particularly relevant to the next chapter, focusing on the impact of our symbiotic microbes on our health. The degree to which holobionts matter to health is another consequence of evolution’s new narrative.

PART II Why It Matters

5 Biodiversity and Human Health

You are not a drop in the ocean. You are the entire ocean in a drop.

—RUMI, THE ESSENTIAL RUMI, TRANSLATED BY COLEMAN BARKS

Why should anyone care about the narrative for life’s history on Earth? Why shine a brighter light on horizontal evolution’s role and the deep and ongoing integration among species? For many, having life’s story better aligned with the facts of evolution is enough: truth is its own reward. And I agree. No justification for a new narrative is required. However, there is much more than accuracy about life’s history at stake. Narratives and their metaphors provide mental coordinates for mapping observations and ideas. When the mental coordinates change, the context and alignment among ideas change. By reorienting our thinking, new narratives, especially scientific narratives, can open minds, challenge assumptions, and reveal new approaches to problem-solving. In this light, I think evolution’s new narrative has key relevance for our understanding of the broad topic of health. This includes the overlapping realms of human health and environmental health. The new narrative emphasizing integration among species can already be seen in the work of many researchers, physicians, epidemiologists, and environmental scientists, and that can be expected to increase over time. Further, making the connections between health and other species’ activities better known to the public should help where an informed public is needed for adoption of expert recommendations.

Chapters 5 and 6 will focus on the intersection of evolution’s narrative with human health and environmental health, respectively. I make the case that human health depends upon our relationships with many other species. We have coevolved with other species, and many of them are part of a vital support system, sustaining our lives and the environmental resources we need. I call this the symbiotic theory of health (table 5.1). This is complementary to the well-accepted, well-known germ theory of disease, in which specific microbes are linked to specific diseases. Germ theory focuses on the short-term roles of other organisms and species in infectious disease, whereas the symbiotic theory of health considers both short- and long-term roles of other species in human health and well-being.

This is not to say that all our relationships with other species are beneficial, or that the nature of the relationships and particular species involved are constant. They are neither. I am saying that human health is strongly influenced by our relationships with other species as a consequence of our long horizontal coevolution with them, and by our ability to manage change in relationships, whether by mitigating harm or promoting benefits.

Table 5.1. Comparison of two theories

Germ Theory of Disease

Symbiotic Theory of Health

Focus on human disease

Focus on human and environmental health

Some human diseases stem from infection with certain microorganisms (pathogens or germs)

Human health stems from the capacity to recover from disease and injury, and to adapt to change over time; considers both infectious and human microbiome species

No direct focus on environmental health

Environmental health stems from the capacity of the environment to sustain humans and other life-forms over time, and is linked to the ability of humans and other species to adapt to change

Focus on short-term relationships between humans and other species in infectious disease; not explicitly evolutionary

Focus on both short-term and longer-term, coevolutionary, relationships of other organisms and species in human and environmental health; species relationships play out globally, from the present time back to life’s origins

Any worthwhile theory is testable by evaluating its predictions. So, what predictions stem from the symbiotic theory of health? Here are three for consideration in chapters 5 and 6. First, diverse, locally coevolved human microbiomes promote better health than do less diverse microbiomes with species that have not coevolved as directly with particular human populations. These coevolved human microbial communities are predicted to be better at defending against common infections and in performing the regular functions of microbiomes in aiding the host’s metabolism and immune system. Second, major change in the nature of our symbiotic relationships with Earth’s other species, including their declines, extinction, or distribution shifts, can significantly impact human health. Third, there is no static optimum for the nature of the symbioses, because the relationships are evolving, both qualitatively and quantitatively. These predictions are broad and difficult to test. However, they do provide an initial framework for evaluation of ideas. Current evidence, reviewed below, supports these predictions, though more evidence is needed, and exceptions can be expected as the complex systems involved become better known.

But first things first. What do we mean by health? Human health is often mistaken to be the mere absence of illness. That view may work on a short time scale. But over longer time frames, health and sickness are not mutually exclusive conditions. Reacting to a flu virus with a fever, aching joints, nausea, and swollen lymph glands, the classic signs of sickness, is a sign of good health, as manifest by a healthy immune system. Sickness and health coexist, and a long life includes many periods of injury and sickness, followed by recoveries. Individuals coping with limiting physical problems can lead otherwise healthy and productive lives. Aging itself, along with the physical and mental changes it brings, is part of a healthy life. At least for now. We have no antidote for aging; however, life-spans are increasing, and some researchers are in pursuit of further delaying, if not stopping, the damaging effects of aging.

Human populations suffering high levels of disease and mortality can persist and experience reduced levels of disease in the future by managing susceptibility to pathogens in some way. Because health and sickness vary over time, there is no narrow or statistically defined normal or abnormal state for these conditions. Environmental health is similarly dynamic. At all levels, health is more about resilience, adaptability to trauma, and persistence over time than it is about absence of sickness. Seeing that life-forms are mutually integrated over time, with species helping shape each other’s environments and evolution, helps us understand and foster both human and environmental health.

A prescient writer on this topic was French philosopher and physician Georges Canguilhem. He wrote in the 1940s about disease and health as defying precise definition. He wrote of human physiology: “There is no biological science of the normal. There is a science of biological situations and conditions called normal. Man is healthy insofar as he is normative [meaning functional or viable] relative to the fluctuations of his environment.” He also remarked that “diversity is not disease; the anomalous is not the pathological.”1 This is all to say that health is not a fixed condition. Being healthy is an ability to be ill and recover. It is being able to meet one’s functional needs over time by being resilient and adapting to change.

Canguilhem was reacting to the narrow definitions of health and norms at the time. He was not discussing health in explicitly evolutionary terms, but his long-term views mesh well with evolution’s new narrative focusing on integration among species. Being healthy includes adaptation to frequent microbial infection. As we’ll see, these adaptations range from strengthening the immune system to hosting microbes with important functions that we are just beginning to perceive. We can view the unique events of rapid horizontal evolution as “normative” in Canguilhem’s sense. That is, they can provide functional alternatives contributing to the long-term resilience, adaptability, and health of organisms of all kinds.

So far I have outlined an idea: the symbiotic theory of health, which encapsulates the relevance of evolution’s new narrative to health. I’ve also discussed what it means to be healthy. Before reviewing examples that show species relationships impacting human health, let’s take a look at the history of our developing knowledge of health and infectious disease. This can help us see how long and difficult the journey to understanding has been.

As far back as the first century BCE, Roman scholar Marcus Varro supposed the existence of “minute creatures … that cannot be discerned with the eye … that enter the body through the mouth and nose and cause serious diseases.”2 In the 1340s, the Mongol army demonstrated a gruesome understanding of person-to-person transmission of plague (now attributed to the bacterium Yersinia pestis) when it catapulted corpses of its plague victims over the walls of the Black Sea port of Caffa as part of its siege of the city. More humanely, records from the 1370s show that some Aegean seaports required newcomers to spend 30 days in quarantine, isolated on offshore islands, to see if symptoms of plague developed prior to being allowed to mingle with the locals.

The sixteenth-century Italian physician and army general Girolamo Fracastoro tended many victims of bubonic plague, smallpox, typhus, and syphilis, and is credited as the first to formulate a theory explaining human sickness akin to what has become germ theory of disease. This credit is based on his writings Syphilis sive morbus gallicus (Syphilis, or The French disease) and De contagione (On contagion), published in 1530 and 1546. Fracastoro clearly posits contagious living entities, which he calls seeds (seminaria), as the physical cause for diseases, with different types of seeds responsible for different diseases. Although his ideas were dismissed at the time because there was no physical evidence of the seeds or germs, he was influential in promoting the search for natural causes and opposing explanations involving the occult.

Progress in linking observable organisms to human disease required new technology. This began with development of the first microscopes in the 1590s, and was taken to a new level in the mid-1600s by the Dutch fabric merchant Antonie van Leeuwenhoek. Through innovative glass processing to make small spherical lenses, he found tiny life-forms to be ubiquitous. He was the first to see and describe single-celled organisms inhabiting samples of lake water, rainwater, saliva, and the feces of frogs and humans. He referred to the organisms he found as “animalcules,” though we would now call them species of bacteria and protozoa. Van Leeuwenhoek’s contemporary Athanasius Kircher examined the blood of plague victims and supposed that the disease was caused by the animalcules he saw there, though he may have been seeing red blood cells rather than the actual bacterial cause of plague. Acting on the readily observed capacity for plague to spread among victims, Kircher made commonsense recommendations, including isolation of the sick, burning their clothes, and wearing face masks to prevent spread of unseen contagious elements.

In the eighteenth century, botanist and physician Carl Linnaeus was also interested in explaining contagious disease. He offered some rudimentary experiments and speculations about this in the course of working with students, in a volume titled Amoenitates academicae (Academic pleasures). Perhaps most relevant is his student John Nyander’s dissertation, Exanthemata viva (roughly translated as “Living skin eruptions”) explicitly proposing microscopic animals as contagious agents for a variety of diseases including measles, smallpox, plague, and syphilis. Still, direct physical evidence of agents was lacking.

Definitive evidence proving germ theory was finally provided by biologists in the late nineteenth century as a result of refinements in microscopy, including the ability to stain and visualize bacteria, and to isolate and grow bacteria from infected individuals. Robert Koch and colleagues were instrumental, demonstrating anthrax to be caused by Bacillus infection, and the linking of six different infectious diseases to six different bacteria. Broad acceptance of germ theory quickly followed its successful application in minimizing the presence of pathogens in people’s daily lives. Within a few decades, rates of infant mortality were down and average life-spans were moving up.

These successes helped inculcate a basic lesson. Microbes are bad. This is continually reinforced by movies, novels, and news stories about deadly viral outbreaks and bacterial superbugs. And of course infectious diseases continue to wreak havoc. But, as we’ll see, this view needs revision, because microbes are not all bad.

We rely on bacteria living inside us for help in food digestion, nutrient uptake, production of vitamins, metabolism of drugs, regulation of the immune system and inflammation, and protection against infection by other microbes. These processes are all complex systems, influenced by coevolution among humans and various microbes. Think of them as ecosystems for bodily function, and our understanding of them is far from complete. Given their importance in the development of antibodies and the long-term evolution of our immune systems, microbes are, as Homer Simpson said of beer, both the cause and cure for many of our problems.

While a classic one-to-one correspondence between microbial species and disease remains true in some instances, in others disease stems from changes in the relative abundance of and interactions among multiple microbe species and their hosts. The concept of a bodily ecosystem pertains here too, as interactions among microbiome constituents, genetics of the host, and the environment are all involved.

Holobionts and Health

All people, like all animals, are accompanied throughout their lives by a swarm of microbes. Not just now, but throughout their species’ evolutionary history. These are long-term evolutionary relationships. Microbes have colonized and adapted for living in the many spaces of human bodies, as birds might colonize and adapt to different environments on a continent. They are at home in our guts, urinary tracts, lungs, and mouths, and across our skin surfaces from scalp to soles. The collective of all microbes living in and on a particular host is known as the microbiome, and it includes bacteria, archaea, fungi, and viruses. A human microbiome collective has about 200 times as many different genes as found in the genome of the host, and it weighs roughly five pounds. We have only recently become aware of the great diversity of species within microbiomes, their changing presence and abundance over time, and their impacts on human health. Our knowledge is rudimentary, but clear evidence is building for correlations and some causation between aspects of human health and microbiome activity and change.

As noted in chapter 4, the assemblage of a host and its microbiome is known as a holobiont. For a human holobiont, this subsumes the human and all its microbes into a single, inclusive entity that can be studied in its own right (figure 5.1). Recognition of holobionts is recognition of the integration of hosts and their microbes, including their potential coevolution. The individual organisms are still a key focus for research, but they can be considered as part of a larger whole, the holobiont. In the context of health, recognizing and studying holobionts is an important step in expanding our knowledge of microbiome species’ impacts on the host’s health.

There is a fascinating debate among evolutionary biologists regarding the extent to which holobionts are acted on by natural selection as units, distinct from the individual organisms involved.3 Given growing recognition that natural selection operates at multiple levels of organization (genes, individual organisms, and groups of organisms), I think the holobiont concept does reflect and enhance our understanding of evolution. Identifying units of selection has always been challenging, as the neat definitions of units become fuzzy when closely examined. Genes, often viewed as targets of natural selection, are hard to define and isolate as potential targets. Some encode proteins, some don’t. Some are contiguous strings of DNA, others are discontinuous though working in concert. Relatively few control single traits or functions (once part of the definition of genes) without assistance. Individual organisms are similarly difficult to define, though this may seem counterintuitive at first. We can recognize a lone wolf but have trouble identifying a single aspen tree, which grows clonally and can have hundreds of trunks. Considering holobionts as targets of evolution may be even more difficult, including a changeable collection of individual microbes of many different species and their genes, all of which are potential subjects of natural selection.

Researchers Ford Doolittle and Andrew Inkpen offer an alternative approach. They suggest that the “collective functions of microbial communities,” rather than the microbial species themselves (individually or collectively), may be the units of selection for holobionts, promoting their persistence over time. They call this theory “It’s the song not the singer,” or ITSNTS.4 That is, the metabolic activities of microbes, key to their host’s health and survival, may be the target for natural selection. Regardless of how this plays out, the associations among organisms and species within a holobiont are important to the health of the host, and for our purposes the concept is apt.

The microbes composing human microbiomes are deeply involved in the life of their host. Some are essential in digesting food. Others produce vital nutrients, regulate the immune system, secrete toxins to protect against pathogens, and even contribute to mental health. Living turns out to be a team effort, even if none of the players are cognizant of the larger effort and its intricate details. Like “seeing the forest for the trees,” seeing the holobiont as a team and not just isolated species is key for aspects of health care. Recognizing that host and microbiome species are, and have long been, integrated as a functional whole improves our ability to understand the key influences for health and disease. It also provides the mindset needed to envision and evaluate treatments for health recovery.

Evidence is growing for the correlation of some diseases with changes in the composition and activities of microbiome species, known as dysbiosis. The diseases include rheumatoid arthritis, obesity, diabetes, inflammatory bowel disease, muscular dystrophy, multiple sclerosis, and the general fatigue and muscle pain of fibromyalgia. And, remarkably, they appear to be inherited along with the microbiome, rather than along with the host’s DNA.5

Obesity rates worldwide have more than doubled since the 1980s; over 100 million children and 600 million adults are now affected. Being obese or overweight (having a body mass index over 30 or 25 respectively) is clearly associated with heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and cancer, which are leading causes of preventable death. Obesity has multiple inducements, with primary risk factors including behavior, genetics, environment, and, to the initial astonishment of many, imbalance within the gut microbiome, noted earlier as dysbiosis.

In a microbiome study of immigrants from Thailand to the United States, gut microbiomes changed in the migrants following their arrival.6 Microbial species diversity began to decline. Some species were lost and replaced by a less diverse and somewhat different set of bacteria typical of the US population. The longer the migrants stayed, the greater the change in their microbiome species composition. Dietary change, use of antibiotics, and increased or “excessive” hygiene have all been implicated in microbiomes with lower species diversity, although how and why these interactions happen remain poorly understood. In the study of Thai immigrants, the microbiome change was even greater in the first generation born in the United States, and it was correlated with increasing obesity and increased cancers of the stomach, liver, and kidney.7

Evidence for the connection between obesity and gut microbiome dysbiosis suggests that obesity might be treated by adjusting the gut microbiota. There is active debate about how to do this. However, researchers have found that, in some instances, supplementing diets with plant fiber stimulating growth of beneficial gut bacteria (prebiotics) was helpful in obese children, by increasing populations of several beneficial bacteria and lowering fat deposition and body weight.8

Microbiome dysbiosis can impact behavior as well. Gut bacteria produce chemical compounds that pass into the bloodstream, and from there they can and do stimulate the nervous system and brain, influencing the host’s mood and behavior. Breeding experiments with mice that have been given microbiomes of differing species composition have found that genes from the host and from the host’s gut bacteria collaborate in regulating anxiety and epilepsy. These host behaviors were previously thought to be controlled by the host’s genes alone. Although the genetic systems involved in mice are not identical to those in humans, they are similar, and the hunt is on for similar evidence in humans. The mouse experiments also showed that restoring a particular microbial species (Lactobacillus reuteri) resulted in reversal of some anxiety and epileptic behaviors.9

Other connections between microbial activity and mammalian brain functions, beyond species composition of microbiomes, are becoming better known. Experiments, again mostly in mice, have shown the ways in which the microbiome might be involved in human Parkinson’s disease. The gut bacterium Escherichia coli makes a protein named curli, which can prompt other proteins, including alpha-synuclein, to misfold. This is a big deal, as misfolding changes the protein’s three-dimensional structure and can wreak havoc with protein function. Some researchers have found evidence that the misfolded alpha-synuclein proteins transmit the error to the brain, where misfolded alpha-synuclein appears linked to symptoms of Parkinson’s disease. Someday, identifying microbial activities impacting human brain disorders like this might lead to therapeutic options that target the microbiome.10

Humans, like all animals, have immune systems as an evolved and necessary response to pathogens. I say necessary because to the world’s infectious bacteria, protists, and viruses, humans loom as rich resources for their reproduction. We provide convenient circulating nutrients, abundant surfaces and cavities for occupation, and we gather in dense communities with alluring dispersal opportunities for their many descendants. We rely, absolutely, on our immune systems for defense. To prove the point, children born with the rare genetic disorder known as severe combined immunodeficiency, or SCID, lack functional immune systems and, without early intervention, usually die in their first year after suffering recurrent infectious diseases including ear infections, pneumonia, and chronic diarrhea.

Some immune system components are present at birth; other components, including those of the adaptive immune system in vertebrate animals, are developed or learned over time. The adaptive immune system includes the capacity to remember particular pathogens and generate specific protective proteins (antibodies) to identify and eliminate them when necessary. Adaptive immune systems require some experience with infectious organisms to get up to speed. Much of this experience comes early in life, either by inheritance of the mother’s microbes in utero or from the environment.

We make vaccines that partner with our adaptive immune systems, stimulating production of antibodies in preparation for future infectious encounters. Yes, microbes can kill, but they are also the driving force for the evolution and ongoing maintenance of our invaluable immune systems. The human immune system is a clear testament to our integration and coevolution with other species. In many ways, managing human health is a matter of managing the relationships between humans and microbes.

Let’s consider allergies as an instructive example. Allergies are overreactions by the immune system in some people to substances that are typically harmless in others, like particular foods and plant pollens. Allergies are autoimmune disorders. The victim’s immune defenses are, mistakenly, attacking its own tissues. Allergies are on the rise worldwide and especially so in developed countries. Some estimates put the increase at 50 percent globally over the past 20 years. About 15 percent of the US population currently experiences allergies of one kind or another.11 Research points to changes in our lifestyle and environment, especially in early childhood, as primary causes. This includes changes in exposure to microbes and other antigens (substances triggering an immune system response), diet, use of antibiotics, weaning and birthing practices, and their cumulative impacts on human microbiomes.

An association between allergy and exposure to different environments has been recognized at least since the 1860s, when hay fever was described as “almost wholly confined to the upper classes of society” in England, and rarely encountered among farmers.12 The cause was unknown, but suspicions were rightly focused on environments such as hay fields, experienced by one group but not another. Current correlations found between reduced exposure to microbes and increasing allergies in developed countries are cited in support of the hygiene hypothesis. This supposes that reduced exposure to antigens results in reduced production of antibodies, and therefore more allergic overreaction to harmless substances. This idea makes sense, but there are additional factors influencing allergies that need to be considered, such as urbanization and pollution. Many feel that name “hygiene hypothesis” gives a mistaken impression that hygiene generally is the problem. Yes, hygiene can be overdone, and living in a near-sterile environment is not a good idea. However, basic personal hygiene remains important. The hygiene hypothesis has also been known as the old friends hypothesis and the biome depletion hypothesis, each denoting loss of parasites and their stimulus of antibody production, which humans have coevolved with until recent times. These include loss of some beneficial multicellular parasites, such as helminth worms. Some helminths have coevolved with humans as persistent intestinal parasites, and their declining prevalence in people from more developed countries correlates with increased inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. The immune system’s evolved response to parasitic helminths helps dampen inflammation, and that benefit has been lost.

You get the idea. Our immune systems have a coevolved reliance on other species in developing antibodies and antigen tolerances that enhance human health.13 Our relationship with particular microbes may change over time, from pathogenic before antibody development to benign afterward, then back to pathogenic if the microbe evolves and eludes existing antibodies. Though often described as an arms race between pathogens and immune systems, our relationships with infectious microbes are better viewed as shifting along a continuum over time.14 Given the human health benefits of many of our symbionts and microbiome species, it seems possible that regulating and managing helpful microbes may have been as important as fighting harmful ones in the evolution of human immune systems. Evolution’s new narrative helps scientists and the public see health as influenced by our coevolution with other species.

Managing Health

Recognizing our lives as integrated by evolution with other species is an important step in learning about the strengths and weaknesses of our immune systems. We may drive some pathogens to extinction, as with the smallpox virus, or nearly so, as with the polio virus. But our vulnerability to the phenomena of infectious disease will long remain as the flip side to the mutability and innovation of pathogens. Their high rate of reproduction coupled with frequent mutation often puts pathogens at an advantage. Some viruses can produce as many 100,000 offspring per host cell, beginning just eight hours after infection. That allows a great deal of variation and novelty among progeny without risking failure of the parent’s overall reproductive effort. We can best reduce our vulnerability by learning about pathogens’ evolutionary origins and habits, and by minimizing their opportunities to spread and boosting our immunity to them. This is what behavioral modification and vaccines do.

The reality of host-pathogen coevolution helps explain the difficulty of eliminating particular infectious diseases. Pathogens may present moving targets and vaccines take time to develop and apply. Building immunity within human populations and across our species is put at risk by some opting out of compliance with best public health practices. Evolvability of pathogens and the variability of human behavior underlie the challenge for predicting pathogen spread and virulence. The future evolution of pathogens depends on the chance events of mutation and the response of their host populations (figure 5.2).

The world’s diversity of species provides an immense library of molecular- and genetic-engineering outcomes. As we read and compare the genomic books of more and more species, we have greater power to reverse engineer genomes and organisms, by figuring out the evolutionary origins and functions of their components. We can see the widespread sharing, editing, adding, subtracting, and adapting of DNAs across individuals and species. We have gained a basic understanding of the processes and materials used in the design changes.

We are also obsessive tool developers, and have been borrowing and refining the genetic tools and materials as we find them in the library of examples, in order to do some engineering ourselves. Taking the combining of genes from different individuals and species as the crux of genetic engineering, we have been honing methods for mimicking natural horizontal evolution in experimental efforts aiming to improve health since the 1970s. In this light, the value of biological diversity—our library of engineering examples—and horizontal evolution for improving human health is already clear and significant, and it is certain to grow.

We use genetic engineering in attempting to replace defective genes with effective ones in treatments of Parkinson’s disease, some forms of leukemia, and several immune disorders. We insert drug-encoding genes into bacteria to produce insulin, infertility treatments, growth hormones, and monoclonal antibodies for fighting infectious disease. Using mice or other animals as genetically engineered proxies for humans has been vital in learning about many diseases and testing their potential cures. This includes development of vaccines against SARS-CoV-2, HIV/AIDS, flu, and shingles, among others, and development of drugs to treat cancers, malaria, diabetes, mental health disorders, symptoms of aging, and to prevent rejection of transplanted organs. Other applications include development of therapies to regrow neurons in treatment of spinal cord injuries; therapies to treat cancers and Alzheimer’s disease with stem cells, which are able to grow into many different cell types; and development of surgical techniques from joint replacement to repair of heart defects. Some of these advances in human health have been applied to other species as well, especially in our pets and in work and zoo animals. All of this stems from learning about and mimicking aspects of horizontal evolution.

The involvement of microbiome species in disease suggests a possible role for microbes in disease treatments. Beneficial microbes might be added. Detrimental microbes might be removed or reduced in numbers. But situations are rarely if ever this simple, and efforts along these lines have had only limited success to date. However, research is well underway, and we have much learn about the complex mechanisms involved. Infection with the bacterium Clostridioides difficile can cause severe diarrhea, fever, and stomach pain. This often arises following use of antibiotics, which disrupt the gut microbiome, causing dysbiosis. It has been identified as one of the most frequent causes of health-care-associated infection and death in hospitalized patients.15 Several studies show the promise of an approach for treatment of C. difficile infection called fecal microbiota transplant (FMT), or stool transplant, which uses pills to transfer fecal bacteria found in healthy individuals to sick individuals. In one study of nine children, seven were in remission and recovering within two weeks of FMT. A similar study focused on FMT treatment for irritable bowel disease, more descriptively known as spastic bowel, found improvements in the quality of life among the 14 adult patients after treatment.16 However, don’t try this at home. FMT remains an experimental, unreliable procedure.

Other approaches to maintaining healthy microbiomes include dietary supplements of prebiotics and probiotics. Prebiotics are indigestible compounds such as fibrous chicory root, which can promote growth of beneficial microbes. Probiotics are foods with live microbes in them. These are most often fermented dairy products like yogurt and kefir. Though promising, there is not yet sufficient evidence to be certain of health benefits. Given the array of diseases now known to be connected to the microbiome, ranging from brain and gut disorders to obesity and cancer, the potential benefits for treating the microbiome and its species composition is high.

I mentioned earlier the benefit of intestinal helminth worms in reducing inflammation and autoimmune disease in humans. A further possibility is that loss of helminths and other symbionts coevolving with humans contributes to the inflammation associated with aging, known as inflammaging. Experimental treatment of mice with a glycoprotein (ES-62) secreted by a parasitic helminth worm has been found to reduce inflammation and slow down their aging processes. This is tantalizing, but only a preliminary step in the potential development of human treatments to increase longevity.17

If parasitic helminths reduce inflammaging in humans, there should be lower incidence of associated diseases such as rheumatoid arthritis where helminth infections are prevalent. There is indeed some evidence for this. In a region of eastern India with high levels of helminth infections, none of the 207 arthritis patients tested positive for helminths, whereas 40 percent of a random sampling of 222 healthy residents in the same region did show evidence of helminth infection. This is matched by recent experimental evidence showing that helminth protein treatments for humans do reduce the incidence of rheumatoid arthritis.18

Appreciating the vital role of other species in human health stems from a series of recent advances. During the past 40 years, biologists have discovered a legion of new kinds of organisms. Roughly 20,000 new species are described annually in publications, and many more are found that are not formally named and described. Most of the new species discovered are bacteria, archaea, protists, or viruses, and they are being found in virtually all habitats surveyed, from the ocean depths to deserts to mountain tops to frozen lakes to body cavities of animals. We are still learning about the scope and mechanisms of their function in the ecosystems where they live. Soils, air, water, and host organisms are all rich in microbial species, and despite the increasing number of surveys, the discovery of new kinds continues.

This discovery of new microorganisms is, in turn, being driven by advances in molecular biology, DNA sequencing, and the field of bioinformatics, which includes development of statistical methods and software tools for analysis of increasingly large molecular datasets. Even though many newly found microorganisms are known only from DNA sequences, information on where they occur and their genetics helps us understand aspects of their evolution and lifestyle.

Understanding that human health is linked to relationships with other species requires recognizing humans as part of nature, not above or apart from nature. But it’s much more than that. Seeing that we are part of nature is the gateway to recognizing that our bodies, behaviors, immune defenses, and vulnerabilities are all outcomes of coevolution with other species over time. The better we understand the species relationships that brought us to this point, the better we can manage our health if and as those relationships change. Gaining that understanding is the challenge.

Science generally, and evolution science in particular, is an attempt to explain phenomena that cannot be experienced directly by human beings. No one can watch subatomic particles directly or the forces compelling their behavior. No one can observe and experience the flow of genetic information through and across all organisms over geologic time scales. This same invisibility applies to the ancient thicket of long-term relationships among species that have shaped human health and susceptibility to disease. Modern science allows us to see further into the past and have greater penetrance of this thicket than ever before. We can track genes and their functions as they move within and between species. We can experiment to reveal benefits or liabilities of infectious life-forms and understand the fallout from losing or gaining particular microbes as our own diets, behaviors, and environments change.

The importance of our relationships with other species extends well beyond the role of microbiome species on individual humans. As we’ll see in the next chapter, other species are also vital in sustaining healthy environments for humans.

6 Biodiversity and Environmental Health

Nature is not a place to visit. It is home.

—GARY SNYDER, THE PRACTICE OF THE WILD

Evolution’s new narrative clarifies the importance of species relationships for healthy environments. And this accentuates a second component of the symbiotic theory of health. The first component, presented in the previous chapter, considered the evolutionary integration of humans with other species, specifically the microbiome, as an important part of human health. The second component also emphasizes the importance of species relationships, but in the context of environmental health. The latter is defined as the capacity of the environment to sustain humans and other life-forms over time. This capacity is linked to the ability of humans and other species to adapt to change (see table 5.1). In both this and the preceding chapter, I consider coevolution and interactions among species as an indirect form of horizontal evolution, as highlighted in the new narrative for evolution. I put our species in context, here, as one among millions sharing ancestry and sharing the environment. Indeed, these millions of species, including ourselves, compose and help form our environment.

But for many, this context is less than obvious. Most of us are buffered from natural environments, relying on technology for food, shelter, medicines, and travel. Our basic survival skills, stripped of technology, have atrophied, and we live in areas and densities that preclude reliance on hunting and gathering food. Considering environmental health for a nonhuman can help us see more clearly the lengthy coevolution, the current connections, and the large role that other species play in healthy, favorable environments for any species of concern. So, let’s consider the magnificent peregrine falcon. It lives off the land, in a way our species once did but no longer can. Having been a falconer, this species is among my favorites and directly familiar.

Lives in Nature

Peregrines need lots of other species to eat. Though they specialize on small songbirds, more than a fifth of all 10,000 extant bird species have been recorded as peregrine prey somewhere across their global range, varying from hummingbirds to great gray owls, helping, involuntarily, to sustain peregrines as a species (figure 6.1). This is also true for the smaller component of bats, voles, shrews, hares, and other small mammals, as well as reptiles, amphibians, and insects, on which they feed where conditions suit.

Peregrines also need whoever and whatever it is that supports their supporters. Thus, a peregrine pair raising their young on the windswept coastal cliffs of northern California require not only hundreds of individual violet-green swallows, cliff swallows, orange-crowned warblers, least sandpipers, and cinnamon teal to feed their brood, they also need the feeding relationships and the species that support all of their diverse prey. If we follow the feeding relationships for just the cinnamon teal among their prey, we find that peregrines, via the cinnamon teal, also rely on insects such as damselflies, dragonflies, caddisflies, beetles, and midges and dozens of species of marine and freshwater snails, slugs, and clams, as well as many plant species whose parts are eaten by cinnamon teal, including pondweeds, bulrushes, sedges, and salt grasses.

Reliance on these species, which compose the food of one of the peregrine’s food species, is still only a sliver of the food web, which includes more examples of what the eaten eat, as with dragonflies eating gnats eating rotten fruits. And there is also reliance on decomposer species providing soil nutrients for plants. If we consider just the decomposer bacteria making nutrients newly available for the bulrushes in the teal’s diet, we can find hundreds of millions of individual bacteria representing hundreds of species, in just a handful of pond muck.

To expand the reach of these feeding relationships, multiply these local relationships for one prey species, the cinnamon teal, to the thousands of other peregrine prey species, not just in California during the breeding season but throughout the peregrine’s annual range, from Alaska to Tasmania. This gives an idea of the number and diversity of the peregrine falcon’s current supporting relationships.

And change is a constant. Declines in abundance of the peregrine’s prey species and declines in those species supporting their prey can and do influence the distribution, reproduction, and overall health of peregrine populations. Similarly, the story of the peregrine’s decline and recovery shows the impacts of environmental toxins moving through their web of feeding relationships. Human application of pesticides with toxic chemicals (DDT and DDE) starting in the 1940s sought to enhance crop yields in the Americas. But after poisoning insects, the toxins moved into insect-eating songbirds, and from there into songbird-eating peregrines. In peregrines the toxins hampered calcium metabolism, yielding thin eggshells, lower reproductive success, and local extinction of breeding populations in the 1960s–1980s. Recovery followed reduction in pesticide use, protection of remaining populations, and reintroduction of captive-bred peregrines.

Our feeding relationships were once as rich in biological diversity as those of peregrines. Although this may still be the case for a few hunter-gatherer human populations, humans are mostly reliant, instead, on technology and a small number of domesticated plants and animals. Our experiences and our awareness of other species are diminished. This feeds the perception that we are somehow above nature and no longer in need of biological diversity to sustain our lives. It also lets us think we are the alpha and omega of life on Earth. But these views are mistaken, as I will argue below.

Just as we needed to clarify what we meant by human health in the previous chapter, we need to address here what is meant by environmental health. For many agencies and individuals concerned with environmental health, including the World Health Organization, the role of environment in human health is considered primarily from the standpoint of human exposure to chemical toxins, radiation, and pathogens. Most environmental health regulations adopted by government agencies attempt to minimize exposure to these environmental dangers. But there is more to consider regarding environmental health than human exposures.

A better conception for environmental health, in better alignment with evolution’s new narrative, is the capacity for the environment to sustain humans and other life-forms. In this view, environmental health varies over time, as it is linked to the ability of humans and other species to coevolve and adapt to change. This is still consistent with use of the term by public health professionals, and it adds the relationships among humans and other life-forms as key components for health. These relationships among species play out globally, across evolutionary time scales ranging from the present back to life’s origins 3.8 billion years ago, and they include all species regardless of size, lifestyle, or distribution.

Yes, there are a relatively small number of species having direct and utmost importance to human health. However, I justify the broad addition of all other species as recognition that our knowledge of the indirect effects of any particular species on others is incomplete, and that those species with direct influence will change over time and by geographic location. The better conception above is still, admittedly, human-centric. The least biased or speciesist conception would not be centered on healthy environments for humans alone. A nonspeciesist view of environmental health is laudable, though not widely embraced. Perhaps the exploration below may help convince readers of all life’s inherent value, individually and collectively.

The idea that our relationships with other species impact environmental health is not new, though it has been underappreciated. The impacts are often hidden, indirect, and so thoroughly baked into our perceptions of the world that we remain unaware of the connections among species over time. We can find some roots for the new narrative, highlighting integration and mutual influence among species, in the work of German naturalist Alexander von Humboldt. He viewed nature as an integrated whole, and was driven to understand the ways in which the environment might influence plants and animals. In 1796, at the age of 32, Humboldt and three companions set off to climb Mount Chimborazo, an ice-capped, more than 20,000-foot-high extinct volcano in the Ecuadorean Andes. He planned to measure and describe as much of the biology, geology, and climate as possible, to better understand how the forces of nature, biotic and abiotic, are interwoven. They did not reach the summit owing to injuries and storms; however, they did set a record (19,413 feet) for the highest elevation reached by European explorers at the time. More importantly, the Chimborazo climb, combined with his years of travel and collecting organisms in diverse habitats, helped him crystalize a unifying vision of nature.

Humboldt saw the distributional patterns and limits for many species in a new light. Plant genera and species he found growing high in the Andes were similar if not the same as those growing at high elevations in Switzerland and Finland, where he had also traveled. Rhododendron-like plants were common in environmentally similar parts of Venezuela, Switzerland, and America. Pines and oaks could be found at similar latitudes in Mexico and America. But in other instances, very similar plant species could be found in differing zones of altitude and temperature.

So, Humboldt began work on a thought-provoking map, detailing the distribution of animal and plant species by elevation, based on a sketch of Chimborazo and its surrounding regions. Scientific names for hundreds of genera and species were carefully placed, and side panels indicated zones for temperature, humidity, atmospheric pressure, and altitude. The published version of the Chimborazo map folded out to be two feet by three feet in size, making it useful for comparison with observations of mountain peaks and landscapes around the world. He drew lines on this and similar maps connecting places with the same average temperatures, coining the term “isotherms,” and then plotted species ranges on top of them, creating a powerful new approach to understanding species’ environmental habits. Humboldt used this and other new tools in pioneering what became the disciplines of biogeography and ecology, key components in understanding the forces shaping biological diversity.

Humboldt’s broad vision of nature as an integrated whole had another novel feature. It included people. He considered and documented the real-world relationship between humans and nature, as few if any had done earlier. At a time when little thought was given to the exploitation of natural resources like trees and fisheries, Humboldt drew on his global view, noting “the restless activity of large communities of men gradually despoil the face of the earth,” and elsewhere, “the produce of the earth is in fact the sole basis of permanent opulence.”1 Humboldt wrote flowery nineteenth-century prose about his love for nature, as did many other travelers. However, he was empirical and prescient in recognizing that biological diversity was at risk from human activities, and that humans, in turn, were at risk as well. Humboldt understood the natural dependency of humans on other species. Based on his field observations and study of collected specimens, he was an early supporter of transmutation—the general idea that species were mutable and that one could transform into another. Humboldt died in 1859, the same year Darwin published the Origin of Species, but if he were to come back, I think he would not be surprised at all to learn details of the crucial impacts that species can have on their environments, on the viability of other species, and on the environment’s capacity to support people.

Speaking of people and the environment, there are some things we just can’t do without, and soil, air, and water are at the top of the list. They are and have always been basic to human lives. What many of us don’t realize is that living organisms are themselves key to the formation, quality, and maintenance of soil, air, and water. Yet, it’s true; without the activities and by-products of organisms we would not have these essential resources as we know and need them. In their absence, Earth would look more like the Moon and be similarly inhospitable for our species.

To raise public awareness about this dependency on species in nature, biologists and others often refer to the beneficial roles of organisms in maintaining healthy environments as nature’s benefits or ecosystem services. This strikes some as overly materialistic, but in a society driven by economics, discussion of nature’s benefits can better reveal the value of organisms in healthy environments. Our long-term health depends on theirs. Let’s next consider the pragmatic, materialist valuations of nature by the little-known reinsurance industry.

Reinsurance is insurance coverage for insurance companies. In 2020, the world’s largest reinsurer, Swiss Reinsurance Company Ltd., headquartered in Zurich, made the following annual value estimates for a series of nature’s benefits at a global level: nutrient cycling by sea grass valued at USD 1.9 trillion, animal pollination of crops at USD 235–577 billion, first sale of fisheries at USD 362 billion, and coral reef tourism at USD 36 billion. Annual value estimates made for benefits on a countrywide level include marine and freshwater fisheries in Canada valued at CAD 3.4 billion; recreational benefits of forest ecosystems in France at EUR 8.5 billion; income from recreational fishing in Germany at EUR 6.4 billion; air purification from trees and forests in the United States at USD 6.8 billion; water purification by marshes and tidal flats in Japan at JPY 674 billion; and physical and mental-health benefits of nature in the United Kingdom at GBP 2 billion.2 Making these annual estimates is a complicated process and subjective at some level. But it’s a safe bet that when insurance companies pay to insure themselves, they value accuracy. This is only a partial accounting of benefits, and it includes nothing about regulation of climate or the provision of nonfisheries food, building and clothing materials, energy products, and medicine. However, it does make clear the high economic value already placed on nature’s benefits and healthy ecosystems.

In recent years, about 40 percent of human deaths globally have been linked by researchers to environmental degradation due to pollution and changes in soil, air, and water quality. Similarly, the World Health Organization identifies undernutrition of mothers and children and contaminated water as leading risk factors globally for death and disease. The degradation of ecosystem services provided by healthy species populations and human population growth are the ultimate driving forces for these problems. However, the role of organisms in ecosystem services and environmental health is easy to miss, despite being crucial.

Most of the organisms involved are minute, and their actions have impact only when billions of individuals, from thousands of species, contribute over long periods of time. The dynamic relationships among the many species involved result in complex linkages that we are just beginning to discern. And the linkages vary in different places, at different times, and even in different geologic eras. Recognizing these linkages and appreciating the roles of other species in contributing to environmental health requires a perspective that is both long term, which is to say evolutionary, and comprehensive in considering all life-forms, living and extinct.

In the following sections I call attention to a small number of particular species, by name, to try to personalize their roles in environmental health. We can’t appreciate our relationships if we don’t know who we are relating to. Perhaps by lifting the veil on the activities of some species, and keeping the very long arc of evolution in mind, we can better grasp the vital relationships and coevolutionary history we share with other species. Let’s start by considering some representative species involved in making and maintaining soil.

Soil

Soils are a mixture of mineral and organic materials with water and air. The mineral parts are provided by the slow weathering of rocks, which includes mineral extraction by acid-wielding lichens. The organic materials are provided by the waste and decaying body parts of organisms (figure 6.2). Water and air are mixed in by organisms, which penetrate the layers of debris and create open spaces. This is the work of animals burrowing, of plant roots and fungal hyphae growing, and tiny pore formation by microorganisms. Soils are recycling centers, with decaying organisms serially feeding and being replaced by their descendants. Variation is high, and scientists now recognize a dozen different categories depending on their parent rock materials, their ages, the activities of resident organisms, and aspects of climate and topography.

The idea that soil is a timeless element is wrong, as it misses the vital roles of organismal evolution and ecology as well as Earth’s history. Had you been present when life first evolved on Earth 3.8 billion years ago, you wouldn’t find fertile soil, as there would have been no prior mixing of decaying bodies of organisms with eroding rock particulate. Had you been present again in upstate New York just after the glacial retreat 18,000 years ago you’d also find none of today’s fertile soils, as those existing soils were scraped off and removed by the glaciers. Fertile soils, rich in organic compounds, are formed and lost over time, and their formation and maintenance require a diversity of species, and great patience.

Species groups with particular importance for soils, beyond the direct contribution of decaying bodies, are bacteria, protozoans (the single-celled eukaryotes we met earlier), fungi, nematodes, arthropods, and earthworms. In fact, most groups of organisms include species representatives that contribute to fertile soils. Let’s consider examples from these groups, giving a few details of their key contributions to soil health and, by dint of our need for soils, to humans.

Bacteria

Ensifer meliloti (formerly Sinorhizobium meliloti) is a bacterial species inhabiting soils around the world, and can be found living either independently or within specialized root structures, called nodules, of legumes like peas and beans (figure 6.3). E. meliloti individuals invade tiny root hairs of their plant hosts, where they multiply and induce the formation of their nodule homes through a clever set of biochemical reactions. For plants, E. meliloti is a desirable live-in partner because it can turn atmospheric nitrogen gas, which is unavailable to the plant, into ammonia, which is reactive and available for use. Nitrogen fixed by E. meliloti and other bacteria is necessary for plants because nitrogen is a key component in DNA, in protein, in chlorophyll molecules, and in the process of photosynthesis. Even though nitrogen constitutes over 78 percent of Earth’s atmosphere, useable nitrogen can be locally scarce, and in some areas it is the key limiting factor for plants. About 90 different species of bacteria and archaea are known to be capable of nitrogen fixation, like E. meliloti, and, collectively, these microbes are estimated to be responsible for more than 90 percent of Earth’s nitrogen fixation. This makes them key organisms not just for healthy soils and ecosystems but for humans and life in general. The need for useable nitrogen is as old as DNA and proteins themselves, and multicellular organisms have depended on nitrogen-fixing microbes for their entire one-billion-year history.

But soil bacteria do much more than make nitrogen available to plants. The decomposition of dead organisms and their parts also requires a variety of bacterial species that make enzymes to break down carbon compounds and convert the carbon to either carbon dioxide or methane. Additional soil bacteria are important in cycling phosphorus, a life-sustaining nutrient, and still others are active in suppression of plant pathogens. For example, bacterial species in the genus Pseudomonas are closely associated with soils that suppress an economically devastating fungal disease of sugar beets and rice. The Pseudomonas bacteria are pursuing their own reproductive interests in suppressing fungal competitors, but the benefits for plants and ultimately people remain the same.
